# English Form 2, Batches E2-1, E2-2: drafts for review (Lessons 1–76, Terms 1 and 2)

Generated by `npm run review:f2-english` from `content/form-2/english/*.json`. Every question and drawing below was built by the engine.
All notes, teach cards, worked examples and paper tasks are **status: draft**. “Authored key” means a person fixed the
right answer (see `AUTHORED.md`); “computed by rule” means code worked it out (a formula, a conversion, a spelling rule).

Teach mode: cards (idea + example + 1 level-1 check) → worked example → practice (levels 1–3) → the note as a summary.
Writing lessons replace practice with a paper task. Speech-work lessons are deferred (they need audio).

## Sample rendered lesson: Lesson 3 in teach mode

### Lesson 3: Grammar- The Simple Present tense

**Card c3-1** (draft)

> With he, she, it or one person or thing, the Simple Present verb takes -s or -es.
>
> _I take the bus._  
> _My uncle takes the bus._  

Check question: `e2-3-form` · fill the blank, level 1 · computed by rule, reused from practice

> Write the verb in the Simple Present.

Sentence: **She ___ breakfast at six every day. (have)**
Typed answer accepted (case and extra spaces ignored): **has**
- Typed **have** (no-s) → “With she the verb takes -s or -es: has.”

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing word is “has”.  
> She has breakfast at six every day. (have)  

**Card c3-2** (draft)

> Timetables and facts are in the Simple Present too.
>
> _The train to Douala leaves at nine._  
> _The sun rises in the east._  

Check question: `e2-3-c2` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**

> Why is “The market opens at seven on Saturdays.” in the Simple Present?

- ✅ It is a timetable.
- ◻️ It is happening now.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: It is a fixed time, not now._
- ◻️ It happened yesterday.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: It is not past._
- ◻️ It is a habit of one person.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: It is a fixed time for everyone._

Full working after a second miss:

> A fixed opening time is a timetable.  
> So the answer is “It is a timetable.”.  

**Card c3-3** (draft)

> Negative: don't or doesn't + the verb without -s. Question: Do or Does + subject + verb.
>
> _Tabe doesn't take the bus._  
> _Does the bus stop here? — Yes, it does._

- ◻️ isn't  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: With an action verb like fix, use don't or doesn't._
- ✅ don't

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing word is “don't”.  
> Tabe and Ndi don't fix motorbikes.  

**Card c3-4** (draft)

> Adverbs of frequency go before the main verb, but after am, is, are.
>
> _She always takes a taxi._  
> _The bus is never late._  

Check question: `e2-3-adverb` · word order, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Tap the words in the right order. Put the adverb in its place.

Tiles: father · travels · Yaoundé · often · to · My (then “.”)
Answer: **My father often travels to Yaoundé.**

Full working after a second miss:

> The sentence is: My father often travels to Yaoundé.  

**Worked example** (draft)

> Put the adverb in the right place, then make the sentence negative and a question: Bih catches a taxi to school. (usually)

1. The adverb goes before the main verb: Bih usually catches a taxi to school.
2. Negative: doesn't + the verb without -s: Bih doesn't usually catch a taxi to school.
3. Question: Does + subject + verb: Does Bih usually catch a taxi to school?

**Answer:** Bih usually catches a taxi to school. / Bih doesn't usually catch a taxi to school. / Does Bih usually catch a taxi to school?

- ◻️ Does

Practice 5: `e2-3-spot` · spot the error, level 3 · **authored key**

> One sentence has a mistake. Which one?

- ✅ She don't like long journeys.  
  _↳ explanation: With she, use doesn't._
- ◻️ I want a window seat.
- ◻️ We never travel at night.
- ◻️ Mr Tanyi doesn't drive at night.

Full working after a second miss:

> The wrong statement is “She don't like long journeys.”.  
> With she, use doesn't.  

**Summary (the note)**

> We use the Simple Present for habits (“I walk to school”), facts (“Water boils at 100 °C”) and timetables (“The bus leaves at seven”).
> With he, she, it or one person or thing, add -s or -es: he walks, she washes, it carries → carries, have → has.
> Negative: don't / doesn't + the verb without -s: “Tabe doesn't take the bus.”
> Questions: Do / Does + subject + verb: “Does the bus stop here?” — “Yes, it does.”
> Adverbs of frequency (always, usually, often, sometimes, rarely, never) go before the main verb but after be: “She always takes a taxi.” “The bus is never late.”
> Some verbs are not used in the -ing form when they mean a state: know, like, want, need, believe. Say “I know him”, not “I am knowing him”.

## Lesson 1: Speaking and Reading- Talking about the different means of transport and reading a text about transportation

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e2-1 (Talking about means of transport; reading about a journey)

**Note** (draft)

> Ngwa travelled from Buea to Bamenda last Saturday. He bought a ticket at the agency office in the motor park. The bus left at seven o'clock. The driver checked every passenger's seat belt and the conductor loaded the luggage. The bus stopped at a checkpoint and at a roadside market. They arrived in the afternoon, and Ngwa's uncle was waiting for him.
> Talking about transport:
> • How do you travel to Bamenda? — I travel by bus. (by bus, by taxi, by train, by plane; but on foot)
> • How long does the journey take? — About six hours.
> • How much is the fare? — Five thousand francs.
> Public transport is transport everyone can pay to use: buses, taxis, trains, ferries, planes.

**Card c1-1** (draft)

> Read the text about Ngwa's journey. It answers: who, where, how, when and what happened.
>
> _Ngwa travelled from Buea to Bamenda last Saturday. He went to the motor park early in the morning and bought a ticket at the agency office._  
> _The bus left at seven o'clock. The driver checked that every passenger wore a seat belt, and the conductor put the luggage on the roof._  
> _The road was good, but the bus stopped at a police checkpoint and for a short break at a roadside market, where Ngwa bought roasted plantains._  
> _They arrived in Bamenda in the afternoon. Ngwa was tired but happy: his uncle was waiting for him at the park._

**Worked example** (draft)

> Bih goes to Limbe every Saturday by taxi. Write two questions about her journey and her answers.

1. To ask about the means of transport, use How: “How do you go to Limbe?” — “I go by taxi.”
2. To ask about time, use How long: “How long does the journey take?” — “About half an hour.”
3. Remember: by + the means of transport, with no a or the (by taxi); but on foot.

**Answer:** “How do you go to Limbe?” — “I go by taxi.” “How long does the journey take?” — “About half an hour.”

## Lesson 2: Vocabulary- Words related to travel and basic spelling rules

Prerequisites: 1 · Skill: e2-2 (Travel words and basic spelling rules)

**Note** (draft)

> Travel words: a journey (going from one place to another), the departure (leaving), the arrival (coming), the destination (the place you are going to), the timetable (the times of buses or trains), a passenger, the fare, a ticket, luggage, a delay (being late).
> Spelling rules when we add an ending:
> • A word ending in silent e drops the e before -ing: ride → riding, arrive → arriving (but not before -s: arrives).
> • One short vowel + one consonant, stressed at the end: double it: stop → stopping, plan → planned. British English also doubles l: travel → travelling.
> • Consonant + y changes y to i: carry → carries, carried; but vowel + y keeps the y: journey → journeys, delay → delayed.
> • A word ending in -ie changes ie to y before -ing: lie → lying.

> The words of a journey: you leave at the departure time and reach your destination at the arrival time.
>
> _Departure: seven o'clock, from Buea. Destination: Bamenda._  
> _Arrival: two o'clock. A delay means the bus is late._

- journey → **going from one place to another**
- timetable → **a list of the times buses or trains leave and arrive**
- departure → **the time a bus, train or plane leaves**

## Lesson 3: Grammar- The Simple Present tense

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e2-3 (The Simple Present tense)

**Note** (draft)

> We use the Simple Present for habits (“I walk to school”), facts (“Water boils at 100 °C”) and timetables (“The bus leaves at seven”).
> With he, she, it or one person or thing, add -s or -es: he walks, she washes, it carries → carries, have → has.
> Negative: don't / doesn't + the verb without -s: “Tabe doesn't take the bus.”
> Questions: Do / Does + subject + verb: “Does the bus stop here?” — “Yes, it does.”
> Adverbs of frequency (always, usually, often, sometimes, rarely, never) go before the main verb but after be: “She always takes a taxi.” “The bus is never late.”
> Some verbs are not used in the -ing form when they mean a state: know, like, want, need, believe. Say “I know him”, not “I am knowing him”.

**Card c3-1** (draft)

> With he, she, it or one person or thing, the Simple Present verb takes -s or -es.
>
> _I take the bus._  
> _My uncle takes the bus._

Typed answer accepted (case and extra spaces ignored): **takes**
- Typed **take** (no-s) → “With she the verb takes -s or -es: takes.”

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing word is “takes”.  
> She takes the bus to school every day. (take)  

**Card c3-2** (draft)

> Timetables and facts are in the Simple Present too.
>
> _The train to Douala leaves at nine._  
> _The sun rises in the east._

- ◻️ It is a timetable.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: There is no time in it._

Full working after a second miss:

> It is always true: a fact.  
> So the answer is “It is a fact.”.

> Negative: don't or doesn't + the verb without -s. Question: Do or Does + subject + verb.
>
> _Tabe doesn't take the bus._  
> _Does the bus stop here? — Yes, it does._

3. Question: Does + subject + verb: Does Bih usually catch a taxi to school?

**Answer:** Bih usually catches a taxi to school. / Bih doesn't usually catch a taxi to school. / Does Bih usually catch a taxi to school?

## Lesson 4: Writing- Write a paragraph on how you travelled by bus

Prerequisites: 1, 3 · Skill: e2-4 (Writing a paragraph about a bus journey)

**Note** (draft)

> A paragraph is a group of sentences about one main idea. It starts on a new line.
> • The topic sentence comes first and says what the paragraph is about: “Last December I travelled from Kumba to Douala by bus.”
> • The supporting sentences give the details in order: buying the ticket, the departure, what happened on the way, the arrival.
> • Use sequence words: first, then, after that, later, finally.
> • A story about the past is in the Simple Past: I went, I bought, the bus left, we arrived.
> • The last sentence closes the paragraph, for example with how you felt.

**Card c4-1** (draft)

> A paragraph starts with a topic sentence that says what it is about.
>
> _Topic sentence: “Last holiday I travelled to Bafoussam by bus.”_  
> _Not a topic sentence: “Then we stopped for food.” (a detail)_  

Check question: `e2-4-c1` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**

> Which sentence is the best topic sentence for a paragraph about a bus journey?

- ◻️ I like mangoes.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is not about the journey._
- ◻️ Finally we arrived.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That closes the paragraph._
- ✅ Last holiday I travelled from Buea to Bamenda by bus.
- ◻️ Then the bus stopped.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is a detail in the middle._

Full working after a second miss:

> It says what the whole paragraph is about.  
> So the answer is “Last holiday I travelled from Buea to Bamenda by bus.”.  

**Card c4-2** (draft)

> Sequence words show the order of events: first, then, after that, later, finally.
>
> _First I bought a ticket. Then I found a seat._  
> _After that the bus left. Finally we arrived._  

Check question: `e2-4-order` · ordering, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Put the sentences of the paragraph in order.

Shown as: Last Saturday I travelled to Bamenda by bus · First I bought my ticket at the agency · Finally we arrived in the afternoon · After that the bus left the park · Then the conductor loaded my bag
Correct order: Last Saturday I travelled to Bamenda by bus → First I bought my ticket at the agency → Then the conductor loaded my bag → After that the bus left the park → Finally we arrived in the afternoon

Full working after a second miss:

> The correct order is: Last Saturday I travelled to Bamenda by bus → First I bought my ticket at the agency → Then the conductor loaded my bag → After that the bus left the park → Finally we arrived in the afternoon.  

**Card c4-3** (draft)

> A story about a past journey is in the Simple Past.
>
> _I went to the motor park and bought a ticket._  
> _The bus left at eight and we arrived at noon._  

Check question: `e2-4-c3` · fill the blank, level 1 · computed by rule

> Write the verb in the Simple Past.

Sentence: **Last week we ___ to Limbe by bus. (fly)**
Typed answer accepted (case and extra spaces ignored): **flew**
- Typed **fly** (present) → “The journey was last week: use the past, flew.”
- Typed **flyed** (ed) → “Check the past form: flew.”

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing word is “flew”.  
> Last week we flew to Limbe by bus. (fly)  

**Worked example** (draft)

> Plan a paragraph about a bus journey from Kumba to Douala.

1. Topic sentence: Last December I travelled from Kumba to Douala by bus.
2. Details in order, with sequence words: First I bought my ticket. Then the bus left. After that we stopped at a checkpoint.
3. Closing sentence: Finally we arrived, and I was happy to see my cousins.

**Answer:** Topic sentence → details in order (first, then, after that) → closing sentence, all in the Simple Past.

**Paper task** (draft; not marked by the app, mastery stays unknown)

> Write one paragraph (six to eight sentences) in your exercise book about a journey you made by bus. Start with a topic sentence, give the events in order with sequence words, use the Simple Past, and end with how you felt.

Model answer, shown after the pupil has written:

> Last December I travelled from Kumba to Douala by bus. First my father took me to the motor park and bought my ticket. Then the conductor put my bag on the roof and I found a seat by the window. After that the bus left, and I watched the plantations go past. On the way we stopped at a checkpoint, and later at a market where I bought groundnuts. Finally we arrived in Douala in the evening. I was tired, but I was happy to see my cousins waiting for me.  

Self-check list:

- [ ] My paragraph starts with a topic sentence.
- [ ] The events are in the order they happened.
- [ ] I used sequence words (first, then, after that, finally).
- [ ] My verbs are in the Simple Past.
- [ ] I wrote by bus, by taxi (not by the bus).
- [ ] My last sentence closes the paragraph.
- [ ] Every sentence starts with a capital letter and ends with a full stop.

Correct order: Last Saturday I travelled to Bamenda by bus → First I bought my ticket at the agency → Then the conductor loaded my bag → After that the bus left the park → Finally we arrived in the afternoon

Full working after a second miss:

> The correct order is: Last Saturday I travelled to Bamenda by bus → First I bought my ticket at the agency → Then the conductor loaded my bag → After that the bus left the park → Finally we arrived in the afternoon.

## Lesson 5: Speaking and Reading- Talking about a health professional and read an interview between a journalist and a dentist

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e2-5 (Health professionals; reading an interview with a dentist)

**Note** (draft)

> Health professionals are people trained to care for our health:
> • a doctor treats illnesses; a nurse cares for patients and helps the doctor;
> • a dentist cares for teeth and gums; an optician tests eyes for glasses;
> • a pharmacist prepares and sells medicines; a midwife helps women give birth;
> • a physiotherapist helps people move again after an injury.
> In the interview, a journalist asked a dentist questions. The dentist said we should visit a dentist twice a year, that sugar causes tooth decay, and that we should brush in the morning and before we sleep.
> At a clinic, say what is wrong: “I have a toothache.” “My eyes hurt when I read.” The professional may ask: “How long have you had it?”

**Card c5-1** (draft)

> Read the interview between a journalist and a dentist.
>
> _Journalist: Good morning, doctor. What does a dentist do?_  
> _Dentist: I take care of people's teeth and gums. I check them, clean them, fill holes and sometimes take out bad teeth._  
> _Journalist: How often should we visit a dentist?_  
> _Dentist: Twice a year, even when nothing hurts. A small problem is easier to treat._  
> _Journalist: What causes tooth decay?_  
> _Dentist: Sugar. Sweets and soft drinks feed the germs that make holes in our teeth. Brush in the morning and before you sleep._  

Check question: `e2-5-c1` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**

> From the interview: When should we brush our teeth?

- ◻️ only after lunch  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The dentist said morning and night._
- ◻️ once a week  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The dentist said every morning and night._
- ◻️ only when they hurt  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: We brush to keep them healthy._
- ✅ in the morning and before we sleep

Full working after a second miss:

> The dentist said in the morning and before you sleep.  
> So the answer is “in the morning and before we sleep”.  

**Card c5-2** (draft)

> Each health professional has a special job.
>
> _A dentist cares for teeth; an optician tests eyes._  
> _A pharmacist sells medicines; a midwife helps women give birth._  

Check question: `e2-5-who` · matching, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Match each health professional to the job.

Right-hand side (shuffled): prepares and sells medicines · cares for teeth and gums · helps women give birth · tests eyes and makes glasses
- optician → **tests eyes and makes glasses**
- pharmacist → **prepares and sells medicines**
- dentist → **cares for teeth and gums**
- midwife → **helps women give birth**

Full working after a second miss:

> The right pairs are:  
> optician → tests eyes and makes glasses  
> pharmacist → prepares and sells medicines  
> dentist → cares for teeth and gums  
> midwife → helps women give birth  

**Card c5-3** (draft)

> At a clinic, say clearly what is wrong, and answer the questions.
>
> _“I have a headache.” “My ear hurts.”_  
> _“How long have you had it?” — “Since yesterday.”_  

Check question: `e2-5-say` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> You are at the clinic. Your tooth hurts. What do you say?

- ◻️ I have a stomach ache.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: A stomach ache is in the stomach._
- ◻️ I have a headache.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: A headache is in the head._
- ◻️ My ear is fine.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That does not say what is wrong._
- ✅ I have a toothache.

Full working after a second miss:

> A pain in a tooth is a toothache.  
> So the answer is “I have a toothache.”.  

**Worked example** (draft)

> Manka has a toothache. Write what she says to the receptionist, and what the dentist may ask her.

1. To the receptionist: “Good morning. I have a toothache. Can I see the dentist, please?”
2. The dentist asks where and how long: “Which tooth hurts? How long have you had the pain?”
3. Manka answers clearly: “The back tooth on the left. Since Monday.”

**Answer:** “I have a toothache. Can I see the dentist, please?” — “Which tooth hurts? How long have you had the pain?”

## Lesson 6: Vocabulary- Dental healthcare vocabulary

Prerequisites: 5 · Skill: e2-6 (Dental healthcare words)

**Note** (draft)

> Parts of the mouth: teeth (one tooth), gums (the pink flesh around the teeth), the tongue.
> Kinds of teeth: incisors (front teeth for cutting), canines (pointed teeth for tearing), molars (back teeth for chewing and grinding).
> Cleaning: a toothbrush, toothpaste, dental floss (thread to clean between the teeth), mouthwash.
> Problems: tooth decay or a cavity (a hole in a tooth), toothache (pain), bleeding gums, bad breath.
> Treatment: a check-up (a visit to look at the teeth), a filling (the dentist fills the hole), an extraction (the dentist takes the tooth out), braces (wires that straighten teeth).
> Plural: one tooth, two teeth.

**Card c6-1** (draft)

> Teeth have different jobs: incisors cut, canines tear, molars grind.
>
> _We bite into a mango with our incisors._  
> _We chew meat with our molars._

3. If the tooth is too damaged, the dentist does an extraction: he takes it out.

## Lesson 7: Grammar- The Present continuous tense

Prerequisites: 3 · Skill: e2-7 (The Present Continuous tense)

**Note** (draft)

> We use the Present Continuous for actions happening now (“The nurse is checking my temperature”), for things happening around now (“I am reading a book about health this week”) and for plans already fixed (“I am seeing the dentist on Friday”).
> Form: am / is / are + verb-ing. I am waiting. She is waiting. They are waiting.
> Spelling: write → writing, sit → sitting, lie → lying.
> Negative: am not / isn't / aren't + -ing: “He isn't sleeping.”
> Question: Am / Is / Are + subject + -ing: “Are you coming?” — “Yes, I am.”
> State verbs (know, like, want, need, believe, own) are not used with -ing: say “I need a doctor”, not “I am needing”.
> Signal words: now, right now, at the moment, today, this week, Look!, Listen!

**Card c7-1** (draft)

> The Present Continuous is am, is or are + the verb with -ing.
>
> _I am waiting for the doctor._  
> _She is waiting for the doctor. They are waiting for the doctor._  

Check question: `e2-7-form` · fill the blank, level 1 · computed by rule, reused from practice

> Write the verb in the Present Continuous.

Sentence: **Ayuk ___ the waiting room now. (clean)**
Typed answer accepted (case and extra spaces ignored): **is cleaning**
- Typed **cleaning** (no-be) → “Add is before the -ing form.”
- Typed **are cleaning** (wrong-be) → “With Ayuk, use is.”

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing words are “is cleaning”.  
> Ayuk is cleaning the waiting room now. (clean)  

**Card c7-2** (draft)

> Use it for what is happening now, and for a plan already fixed.
>
> _Look! The doctor is coming._  
> _I am seeing the dentist on Friday. (a fixed plan)_

- ✅ is visiting
- ◻️ visited  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Next Tuesday is in the future._
- ◻️ was visiting  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is a past form._

Full working after a second miss:

> A fixed plan uses the Present Continuous.  
> So the answer is “is visiting”.  

**Card c7-3** (draft)

> Negative: am not, isn't, aren't + -ing. Question: Am, Is, Are + subject + -ing.
>
> _He isn't sleeping._  
> _Are you coming to the clinic? — Yes, I am._

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing word is “are”.

**Card c7-4** (draft)

> State verbs like know, like, want, need and believe do not take -ing.
>
> _I need a doctor. (not: I am needing)_  
> _She knows the nurse. (not: she is knowing)_

Full working after a second miss:

**Worked example** (draft)

> Write a sentence in the Present Continuous: The nurse / take / my temperature / now. Then make it negative and a question.

1. The nurse is one person: is + taking: The nurse is taking my temperature now.
2. Negative: isn't + taking: The nurse isn't taking my temperature now.
3. Question: Is + subject + taking: Is the nurse taking my temperature now?

**Answer:** The nurse is taking my temperature now. / The nurse isn't taking my temperature now. / Is the nurse taking my temperature now?

## Lesson 9: Speaking and Reading- Talking about pandemics and other contagious diseases and their treatment

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e2-9 (Talking about pandemics and contagious diseases; reading about an outbreak)

**Note** (draft)

> A contagious disease passes from one person to another, for example cholera, measles, tuberculosis and COVID-19. An epidemic is when many people in one area catch a disease at the same time; a pandemic is an epidemic that spreads across many countries, like COVID-19 in 2020.
> In the text, cholera spread through dirty water and food. The sick lost water through diarrhoea and vomiting. They were treated with oral rehydration salts (ORS) and in hospital. Families boiled their water, washed their hands with soap and used latrines.
> Malaria is a serious illness, but it is not contagious: mosquitoes carry it from person to person, so we sleep under a mosquito net.
> Talking about illness: “What are the symptoms?” “How does it spread?” “How can we prevent it?”

**Card c9-1** (draft)

> Read the text about a cholera outbreak.
>
> _Last rainy season there was an outbreak of cholera in a town near the coast. Cholera is a contagious disease: it spreads when people drink dirty water or eat food that is not clean._  
> _The sick had watery diarrhoea and vomited, so their bodies lost a lot of water. Nurses gave them oral rehydration salts (ORS) mixed with clean water, and the very sick were treated in the hospital._  
> _Health workers went from house to house. They told families to boil or treat their drinking water, to wash their hands with soap and to use latrines. After a few weeks, the outbreak ended._  

Check question: `e2-9-c1` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**

> From the text about the cholera outbreak: Why did the sick need ORS?

- ✅ Their bodies lost a lot of water.
- ◻️ They had a cough.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The text does not talk about a cough._
- ◻️ They could not sleep.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The text does not say that._
- ◻️ They were hungry.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The text talks about losing water._

Full working after a second miss:

> Diarrhoea and vomiting made them lose water.  
> So the answer is “Their bodies lost a lot of water.”.  

**Card c9-2** (draft)

> Contagious means it can pass from one person to another. An epidemic spreads in one area; a pandemic spreads across many countries.
>
> _Cholera, measles and COVID-19 are contagious._  
> _Malaria is not contagious: mosquitoes carry it._  

Check question: `e2-9-words` · matching, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Match each word to its meaning.

Right-hand side (shuffled): a sign of an illness, such as a fever · many people in one area catch a disease at the same time · stopping something before it happens · can pass from one person to another
- epidemic → **many people in one area catch a disease at the same time**
- prevention → **stopping something before it happens**
- symptom → **a sign of an illness, such as a fever**
- contagious → **can pass from one person to another**

Full working after a second miss:

> The right pairs are:  
> epidemic → many people in one area catch a disease at the same time  
> prevention → stopping something before it happens  
> symptom → a sign of an illness, such as a fever  
> contagious → can pass from one person to another  

**Card c9-3** (draft)

> To talk about an illness, ask about the symptoms, how it spreads, and how to prevent or treat it.
>
> _What are the symptoms? — A high fever and a cough._  
> _How can we prevent it? — Wash your hands with soap._  

Check question: `e2-9-ask` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Someone asks: “How can we prevent cholera?” What is the best answer?

- ◻️ Diarrhoea and vomiting.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Those are symptoms._
- ◻️ Through dirty water.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is how it spreads._
- ✅ Drink clean water and wash our hands with soap.
- ◻️ Near the coast.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That answers Where._

Full working after a second miss:

> Prevent asks what we do to stop it.  
> So the answer is “Drink clean water and wash our hands with soap.”.  

**Worked example** (draft)

> Answer three questions about cholera from the text: How does it spread? What are the symptoms? How can we prevent it?

1. How it spreads: people drink dirty water or eat food that is not clean.
2. Symptoms: watery diarrhoea and vomiting, so the body loses water.
3. Prevention: boil or treat drinking water, wash hands with soap, use latrines.

**Answer:** It spreads through dirty water and food; it causes diarrhoea and vomiting; we prevent it with clean water, handwashing and latrines.

**Practice** `e2-9-words` · matching, level 1 · **authored key**

> Match each word to its meaning.

Right-hand side (shuffled): a disease that spreads across many countries · stopping something before it happens · many people in one area catch a disease at the same time · can pass from one person to another

## Lesson 10: Vocabulary- Words related to pandemics-Matching healthcare specialists to diseases

Prerequisites: 9 · Skill: e2-10 (Words about pandemics; health specialists and diseases)

**Note** (draft)

> Words about pandemics: a vaccine (an injection that protects against a disease), vaccination, quarantine (keeping people apart who may be sick), isolation (keeping a sick person alone), a face mask, hand sanitiser, social distancing, infection, recovery (getting well again).
> Specialists and what they treat:
> • a dentist: tooth decay and gum disease;   • an ophthalmologist: diseases of the eyes;
> • a dermatologist: skin diseases;   • a paediatrician: illnesses of children;
> • a cardiologist: heart disease;   • a gynaecologist: women's health and pregnancy;
> • an ENT doctor: the ear, nose and throat.
> Many specialist names end in -ist or -ian.

> _A sick person stays in isolation._

## Lesson 11: Grammar- The Simple Past tense

Prerequisites: 3 · Skill: e2-11 (The Simple Past tense)

**Note** (draft)

> We use the Simple Past for finished actions at a time in the past: yesterday, last week, in 2020, two days ago.
> Regular verbs add -ed: visit → visited, stay → stayed, stop → stopped, carry → carried, arrive → arrived.
> Irregular verbs have their own past forms: go → went, take → took, see → saw, feel → felt, buy → bought, catch → caught, drink → drank.
> The past form is the same for every person: I went, she went, they went.
> Negative: didn't + the base verb: “She didn't go to school.” (not: didn't went)
> Questions: Did + subject + base verb: “Did you take the medicine?” — “Yes, I did.”
> Be: I/he/she/it was; you/we/they were.

**Card c11-1** (draft)

> Regular verbs add -ed in the Simple Past. Mind the spelling: stop → stopped, carry → carried.
>
> _visit → visited, stop → stopped_  
> _carry → carried, arrive → arrived_  

Check question: `e2-11-regular` · fill the blank, level 1 · computed by rule, reused from practice

> Write the verb in the Simple Past.

Sentence: **Yesterday the nurse ___ to the village. (travel)**
Typed answer accepted (case and extra spaces ignored): **travelled**
- Typed **travels** (present) → “It was yesterday: use the past, travelled.”
- Typed **traveled** (plain-ed) → “Check the spelling: travelled.”

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing word is “travelled”.  
> Yesterday the nurse travelled to the village. (travel)  

**Card c11-2** (draft)

> Irregular verbs have their own past forms. Learn them by heart.
>
> _go → went, take → took, see → saw_  
> _feel → felt, buy → bought, drink → drank_  

Check question: `e2-11-form` · fill the blank, level 1 · computed by rule, reused from practice

Sentence: **Last week Tabe and Ndi ___ to the hospital. (go)**
Typed answer accepted (case and extra spaces ignored): **went**
- Typed **go** (present) → “It was last week: use the past, went.”
- Typed **goed** (ed) → “Check the past form: went.”

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing word is “went”.  
> Last week Tabe and Ndi went to the hospital. (go)  

**Card c11-3** (draft)

> Negative: didn't + the base verb. Question: Did + subject + the base verb.
>
> _She didn't go to school. (not: didn't went)_  
> _Did you take the medicine? — Yes, I did._

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing word is “was”.

**Worked example** (draft)

> Write in the Simple Past, then make it negative and a question: Ndi / catch / malaria / last month.

1. catch is irregular: its past is caught. Ndi caught malaria last month.
2. Negative: didn't + the base verb: Ndi didn't catch malaria last month.
3. Question: Did + subject + base verb: Did Ndi catch malaria last month?

**Answer:** Ndi caught malaria last month. / Ndi didn't catch malaria last month. / Did Ndi catch malaria last month?

## Lesson 13: Speaking and Reading- Talk about the weather and its unpredictable nature then read a passage on the weather

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e2-13 (Talking about the weather; reading about the seasons)

**Note** (draft)

> Cameroon has a rainy season and a dry season. In the south the rains are heavy; a sunny morning may end with a thunderstorm. In the north the dry season is long and hot, and from about November to February a dry, dusty wind, the harmattan, blows from the Sahara.
> The weather is unpredictable: we cannot always know what it will do.
> Talking about the weather:
> • What's the weather like today? — It's sunny / cloudy / rainy / windy / foggy.
> • It's raining. It's going to rain. The sky is clear.
> • What's the temperature? — About thirty degrees.
> Use it as the subject: “It is hot.” “It rained all night.”

**Card c13-1** (draft)

> Read the text about the seasons in Cameroon.
>
> _Cameroon has two main seasons: the rainy season and the dry season. In the south, the rains are heavy, and on some days a sunny morning ends with a thunderstorm in the afternoon._  
> _In the north, the dry season is long and hot. From about November to February, a dry, dusty wind called the harmattan blows from the Sahara. The sky looks grey, and people's lips and skin become dry._  
> _Farmers watch the weather closely. If the first rains come late, the crops suffer. That is why people say the weather is unpredictable: we cannot always know what it will do._  

Check question: `e2-13-c1` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**

- ◻️ the hot season and the snowy season  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: There is no snowy season in the text._
- ◻️ spring and autumn  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The text names the rainy season and the dry season._
- ◻️ summer and winter  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The text does not talk about summer and winter._
- ✅ the rainy season and the dry season

Full working after a second miss:

> The text says the rainy season and the dry season.  
> So the answer is “the rainy season and the dry season”.  

**Card c13-2** (draft)

> Ask “What's the weather like?” and answer with It is + an adjective.
>
> _What's the weather like? — It's windy and cloudy._  
> _It's raining now. It's going to rain later._  

Check question: `e2-13-reply` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Someone asks: “Is it going to rain?” What is the best answer?

- ◻️ I went to school.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That does not answer._
- ✅ Yes, look at those dark clouds.
- ◻️ The harmattan is dry.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is a fact, not an answer._
- ◻️ It's thirty degrees.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is the temperature._

Full working after a second miss:

> Dark clouds show rain is coming.  
> So the answer is “Yes, look at those dark clouds.”.  

**Card c13-3** (draft)

> Unpredictable means we cannot always say what will happen.
>
> _The weather is unpredictable: a sunny morning can end with a storm._  
> _A predictable bus comes at the same time every day._  

Check question: `e2-13-meaning` · fill the blank, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Choose the word that fits.

Sentence: **The weather is ___: we never know if it will rain.**
- ◻️ dusty  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Dusty describes the air._
- ✅ unpredictable
- ◻️ predictable  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Predictable means we can know._

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing word is “unpredictable”.  
> The weather is unpredictable: we never know if it will rain.  

**Worked example** (draft)

> Describe the weather in Buea on a day that changes: a bright morning and a stormy afternoon.

1. Use it as the subject: It was sunny and warm in the morning, and the sky was clear.
2. Say what changed: In the afternoon dark clouds came, and it began to rain heavily.
3. Add a detail: There was thunder and lightning, and the roads were muddy.

**Answer:** It was sunny in the morning, but in the afternoon it rained heavily, with thunder and lightning.

## Lesson 14: Vocabulary: Weather related words/ Matching vocabulary

Prerequisites: 13 · Skill: e2-14 (Weather words)

**Note** (draft)

> Weather words:
> • Rain: a drizzle (light rain), a shower (short rain), a downpour (very heavy rain), a thunderstorm (rain with thunder and lightning).
> • Wind: a breeze (a gentle wind), a gale (a very strong wind), the harmattan (a dry, dusty wind).
> • Sky: clear, cloudy, overcast (completely grey), foggy or misty (you cannot see far).
> • Temperature: scorching (very hot), warm, cool, chilly (a little cold).
> • Humid means the air is warm and wet. A drought is a long time with no rain. A flood is when water covers dry land.
> Nouns and adjectives: sun → sunny, cloud → cloudy, rain → rainy, wind → windy, fog → foggy, storm → stormy.

**Card c14-1** (draft)

> Rain can be light (a drizzle), short (a shower), very heavy (a downpour) or stormy (a thunderstorm).
>
> _A drizzle hardly wets your clothes._  
> _In a downpour, the gutters overflow._  

Check question: `e2-14-rain` · matching, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Match each word to its meaning.

Right-hand side (shuffled): very light rain · rain with thunder and lightning · a short fall of rain · a very strong wind
- drizzle → **very light rain**
- thunderstorm → **rain with thunder and lightning**
- gale → **a very strong wind**
- shower → **a short fall of rain**

Full working after a second miss:

> The right pairs are:  
> drizzle → very light rain  
> thunderstorm → rain with thunder and lightning  
> gale → a very strong wind  
> shower → a short fall of rain  

**Card c14-2** (draft)

> Add -y to a weather noun to make an adjective: rain → rainy. Double a final consonant after one short vowel: sun → sunny.
>
> _cloud → cloudy, wind → windy_  
> _sun → sunny, fog → foggy_

## Lesson 15: Grammar- The Present Perfect tense

Prerequisites: 11 · Skill: e2-15 (The Present Perfect tense)

**Note** (draft)

> We use the Present Perfect for:
> • past actions whose result matters now: “It has rained, so the ground is wet.”
> • experiences, with ever and never: “Have you ever seen snow?” — “No, I have never seen snow.”
> • actions that started in the past and go on now, with since and for: “It has rained since Monday.” “I have lived here for two years.”
> Form: have / has + past participle. I have seen, she has seen. Participles: visit → visited, see → seen, eat → eaten, write → written, go → gone.
> Negative: haven't / hasn't: “He hasn't finished yet.” Questions: “Have you finished?” — “Yes, I have.”
> Since + a point in time (since Monday, since 2020); for + a length of time (for three days).
> Do not use it with a finished time: say “I saw him yesterday”, not “I have seen him yesterday”.

**Card c15-1** (draft)

> The Present Perfect is have or has + the past participle.
>
> _I have seen a rainbow._  
> _She has seen a rainbow._  

Check question: `e2-15-form` · fill the blank, level 1 · computed by rule, reused from practice

> Write the verb in the Present Perfect.

Sentence: **He ___ the window. (break)**
Typed answer accepted (case and extra spaces ignored): **has broken**
- Typed **broke** (past) → “Use has + the past participle: has broken.”
- Typed **have broken** (wrong-have) → “With he, use has.”
- Typed **has broke** (have-past) → “After has, use the past participle: broken.”

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing words are “has broken”.  
> He has broken the window. (break)  

**Card c15-2** (draft)

> Since + a point in time; for + a length of time.
>
> _It has rained since Monday._  
> _It has rained for three days._  

Check question: `e2-15-since` · fill the blank, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Choose since or for.

Sentence: **It has rained ___ a week.**
- ✅ for
- ◻️ since  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Since goes with a point in time; a week is a length of time, so use for._

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing word is “for”.  
> It has rained for a week.  

**Card c15-3** (draft)

> Use ever in questions and never in negative answers about experiences.
>
> _Have you ever seen snow?_  
> _No, I have never seen snow._  

Check question: `e2-15-have` · fill the blank, level 1 · computed by rule, reused from practice

> Choose the word that fits.

Sentence: **You ___ visited Kribi.**
- ✅ have
- ◻️ is  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The Present Perfect uses have or has._
- ◻️ has  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Has goes with he, she, it and one person or thing._

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing word is “have”.  
> You have visited Kribi.  

**Card c15-4** (draft)

> With a finished time (yesterday, last week, in 2020), use the Simple Past, not the Present Perfect.
>
> _I saw the storm yesterday._  
> _I have seen many storms. (no finished time)_  

Check question: `e2-15-which` · fill the blank, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Choose the correct form.

Sentence: **We ___ in Buea since 2019.**
- ✅ have lived
- ◻️ lived  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Since 2019 goes on to now: Present Perfect._
- ◻️ live  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Since needs the Present Perfect._

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing words are “have lived”.  
> We have lived in Buea since 2019.  

**Worked example** (draft)

> Write in the Present Perfect, then make it negative and a question: Bih / lose / her umbrella.

1. Bih is one person: has + the past participle of lose, lost: Bih has lost her umbrella.
2. Negative: hasn't + participle: Bih hasn't lost her umbrella.
3. Question: Has + subject + participle: Has Bih lost her umbrella?

**Answer:** Bih has lost her umbrella. / Bih hasn't lost her umbrella. / Has Bih lost her umbrella?

## Lesson 16: Writing- Write down 12 words that describe a bright weather

Prerequisites: 13, 14 · Skill: e2-16 (Writing: words for bright weather)

**Note** (draft)

> Descriptive words (adjectives) help the reader see, hear and feel the weather.
> Words for bright weather: sunny, bright, clear, cloudless, blue, warm, hot, scorching, dry, dazzling, golden, pleasant, calm, gentle (breeze), cheerful, sparkling.
> Use your senses: what you see (a clear blue sky), what you feel (the warm sun on my face), what you hear (birds singing).
> Put adjectives before a noun (a cloudless sky) or after be (The sky was cloudless).
> Do not repeat the same word: use a different one (hot, warm, scorching).

**Card c16-1** (draft)

> Adjectives describe nouns. Bright-weather adjectives tell us about the sun, the sky and the air.
>
> _a clear, cloudless sky_  
> _a warm, gentle breeze_  

Check question: `e2-16-c1` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**

> Which word describes bright weather?

- ◻️ overcast  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Overcast is grey._
- ◻️ misty  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Misty means you cannot see far._
- ◻️ gloomy  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Gloomy is dark._
- ✅ cloudless

Full working after a second miss:

> A cloudless sky is bright and clear.  
> So the answer is “cloudless”.  

**Card c16-2** (draft)

> Use your senses: what you see, what you feel, what you hear.
>
> _See: the sparkling river. Feel: the warm sun on my face._  
> _Hear: the birds singing in the mango tree._  

Check question: `e2-16-sense` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which sentence tells what you SEE on a bright day?

- ◻️ The ground was warm under my feet.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is what you feel._
- ◻️ The breeze felt cool.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is what you feel._
- ◻️ Children laughed outside.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is what you hear._
- ✅ Golden light covered the hills.

Full working after a second miss:

> Light on the hills is something you see.  
> So the answer is “Golden light covered the hills.”.  

**Card c16-3** (draft)

> Do not repeat a word: choose another word with a close meaning.
>
> _hot → warm, scorching_  
> _bright → sunny, dazzling_  

Check question: `e2-16-c3` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**

> Which word has a close meaning to “calm”?

- ◻️ stormy  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Stormy is the opposite of calm._
- ✅ still
- ◻️ windy  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Windy is not calm._
- ◻️ noisy  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Noisy is about sound, not calm._

Full working after a second miss:

> Still air is calm.  
> So the answer is “still”.  

**Worked example** (draft)

> Write two sentences about a bright morning on the school field, using at least four describing words.

1. Choose words: bright, clear, warm, golden.
2. Sentence about what you see: The bright sun rose in a clear blue sky over the school field.
3. Sentence about what you feel: The warm, golden light made everyone cheerful.

**Answer:** The bright sun rose in a clear blue sky over the school field. The warm, golden light made everyone cheerful.

**Paper task** (draft; not marked by the app, mastery stays unknown)

> In your exercise book, write down twelve different words that describe bright weather. Then use at least six of them in four sentences about a bright day in your town or village.

Model answer, shown after the pupil has written:

> Words: sunny, bright, clear, cloudless, blue, warm, hot, dry, dazzling, golden, pleasant, calm.  
> It was a bright, sunny morning in Kumba, and the sky was clear and cloudless.  
> The warm sun dried the clothes on the line very quickly.  
> At noon the light was so dazzling that I had to close my eyes.  
> In the evening the air was calm and pleasant, and the sky turned golden.  

Self-check list:

- [ ] I wrote twelve different words.
- [ ] Every word describes bright weather (no rainy or cloudy words).
- [ ] I used at least six of the words in my sentences.
- [ ] I wrote about what I see, feel or hear.
- [ ] I did not repeat the same describing word.
- [ ] My sentences start with a capital letter and end with a full stop.
- [ ] I checked my spelling (sunny with two n's).

## Lesson 17: Speaking and Listening- Talk about telephones and brands of phones

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e2-17 (Talking on the telephone)

**Note** (draft)

> Phones: a mobile phone (cell phone) can call, send text messages, take photos and use the internet; a landline stays in one place. People choose a phone for its price, battery, camera and memory.
> Telephone language:
> • Answer: “Hello, Mount Fako Clinic. How can I help you?”
> • Say who you are: “This is Ebai.” (not “I am Ebai” or “Here is Ebai”)
> • Ask for someone: “May I speak to Nurse Manyi, please?”
> • Wait: “Hold on, please.” / “Please hold.”
> • Messages: “Can I take a message?” “Could you tell her …?”
> • Mistakes: “Sorry, you have the wrong number.”
> • End: “I'll call back later. Goodbye.”
> On the phone, speak clearly and politely, and do not shout.

**Card c17-1** (draft)

> Read this telephone call. (In class you would listen to it.)
>
> _Receptionist: Good afternoon, Mount Fako Clinic. How can I help you?_  
> _Ebai: Good afternoon. May I speak to Nurse Manyi, please?_  
> _Receptionist: Hold on, please … I'm sorry, she's busy at the moment. Can I take a message?_  
> _Ebai: Yes, please. This is Ebai Tanyi. Could you tell her I can't come on Friday? I'll call back tomorrow._  
> _Receptionist: Certainly. I'll give her the message. Goodbye._  
> _Ebai: Thank you. Goodbye._  

Check question: `e2-17-c1` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**

> From the telephone call: Who did Ebai want to speak to?

- ◻️ the doctor  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Ebai asked for Nurse Manyi._
- ◻️ the receptionist  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The receptionist answered the phone._
- ✅ Nurse Manyi
- ◻️ his mother  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Ebai asked for Nurse Manyi._

3. The secretary offers a message: “I'm sorry, he's out. Can I take a message?” — “Yes, please. Could you tell him I called?”

**Answer:** “This is Ngwa. May I speak to Mr Fon, please?” — “I'm sorry, he's out. Can I take a message?”

## Lesson 18: Vocabulary- Telephone and shopping vocabulary

Prerequisites: 17 · Skill: e2-18 (Telephone and shopping words)

**Note** (draft)

> Telephone words: to dial (enter the number), to ring, a missed call, a text message (an SMS), voicemail, airtime or credit (money for calls), a SIM card, a charger, the battery, a network (the signal), to top up (add credit).
> Shopping words: a customer (the buyer), a shop assistant, the price, a discount (money taken off the price), a bargain (a good price), a receipt (the paper that shows you paid), change (money given back), to order (ask to buy), delivery (bringing goods to you), cash or mobile money, out of stock (none left).
> Useful questions: “How much is this phone?” “Do you have a cheaper one?” “Can I pay by mobile money?” “Can I have a receipt, please?”

**Card c18-1** (draft)

> Telephone words: dial a number, top up your credit, charge the battery.
>
> _I topped up my credit to call my aunt._  
> _My battery is low: where is the charger?_

> Match each telephone word to its meaning.

Right-hand side (shuffled): a spoken message left on the phone · a call you did not answer · it fills the battery with power · the signal your phone needs
- voicemail → **a spoken message left on the phone**
- network → **the signal your phone needs**
- charger → **it fills the battery with power**
- missed call → **a call you did not answer**

Full working after a second miss:

> The right pairs are:  
> voicemail → a spoken message left on the phone  
> network → the signal your phone needs  
> charger → it fills the battery with power  
> missed call → a call you did not answer

Full working after a second miss:

**Card c18-3** (draft)

> Polite shopping questions start with How much, Do you have, Can I.
>
> _How much is this charger?_  
> _Can I pay by mobile money?_  

Check question: `e2-18-ask` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> You are in a phone shop. You want proof that you paid. What do you say?

- ◻️ Can I have a charger?  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: A charger is for the battery._
- ◻️ Can I have change?  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Change is money given back._
- ◻️ Can I have a discount?  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: A discount lowers the price._
- ✅ Can I have a receipt, please?

Full working after a second miss:

> A receipt shows you paid.  
> So the answer is “Can I have a receipt, please?”.  

**Worked example** (draft)

> A phone costs 45 000 francs, with a discount of 5000 francs. Tabe pays with a note of 50 000 francs. Use shopping words to explain what happens.

1. The discount is taken off the price: 45 000 − 5000 = 40 000 francs.
2. Tabe pays 50 000 francs, so he gets change: 50 000 − 40 000 = 10 000 francs.
3. He asks: “Can I have a receipt, please?” The receipt shows he paid.

**Answer:** He pays 40 000 francs, gets 10 000 francs change and a receipt.

## Lesson 19: Grammar- The Simple future tense

Prerequisites: 3 · Skill: e2-19 (The Simple Future tense)

**Note** (draft)

> We talk about the future in several ways:
> • will + the base verb, for predictions, promises and decisions made now: “It will rain tomorrow.” “I'll call you tonight.” (I'll = I will)
> • am / is / are going to + the base verb, for plans already made and for what we can see coming: “I'm going to buy a phone on Saturday.” “Look at the clouds: it's going to rain.”
> • Shall I / Shall we, for offers and suggestions: “Shall I carry your bag?” “Shall we go?”
> Will is the same for every person: I will, she will, they will.
> Negative: will not = won't: “He won't come.” Questions: “Will you help me?” — “Yes, I will.”
> After will, use the base verb: “She will buy”, not “She will buys”.

**Card c19-1** (draft)

> The Simple Future is will + the base verb, the same for every person.
>
> _I will call you tonight._  
> _She will call you tonight. (not: will calls)_  

Check question: `e2-19-will` · fill the blank, level 1 · computed by rule, reused from practice

> Write the verb with will.

Sentence: **We ___ you tonight. (call)**
Typed answer accepted (case and extra spaces ignored): **will call**
- Typed **will calls** (will-s) → “After will, use the base verb: will call.”
- Typed **call** (present) → “This is about the future: use will call.”

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing words are “will call”.  
> We will call you tonight. (call)  

**Card c19-2** (draft)

> Use going to for a plan already made, or for something we can see coming.
>
> _I'm going to visit my aunt on Saturday. (a plan)_  
> _Look at those clouds! It's going to rain._  

Check question: `e2-19-which` · fill the blank, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Choose the best form.

Sentence: **I've already decided: I ___ study medicine.**
- ◻️ study now  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: It is a future plan._
- ◻️ studied  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: A plan already made: am going to._
- ✅ am going to

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing words are “am going to”.  
> I've already decided: I am going to study medicine.  

**Card c19-3** (draft)

> Shall I and Shall we make offers and suggestions.
>
> _Shall I open the window?_  
> _Shall we go to the market together?_  

Check question: `e2-19-c3` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**

> Which sentence is an offer to help?

- ✅ Shall I call a taxi for you?
- ◻️ Is the taxi coming?  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is a question about the taxi._
- ◻️ I'm going to take a taxi.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is a plan._
- ◻️ The taxi will be late.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is a prediction._

Full working after a second miss:

> Shall I …? offers help.  
> So the answer is “Shall I call a taxi for you?”.  

**Worked example** (draft)

> Write in the future with will, then make it negative and a question: Manyi / buy / a new phone / next month.

1. will + the base verb, the same for every person: Manyi will buy a new phone next month.
2. Negative: won't + the base verb: Manyi won't buy a new phone next month.
3. Question: Will + subject + the base verb: Will Manyi buy a new phone next month?

**Answer:** Manyi will buy a new phone next month. / Manyi won't buy a new phone next month. / Will Manyi buy a new phone next month?

## Lesson 20: Writing- Write a suitable sentences about online shopping

Prerequisites: 18, 19 · Skill: e2-20 (Writing about online shopping)

**Note** (draft)

> Online shopping means buying goods on the internet, with a phone or a computer, and getting them delivered or collecting them.
> Steps: choose a shop or app, look at the products and prices, put what you want in the cart (basket), order, pay (by mobile money or card, or cash on delivery), and wait for the delivery.
> Advantages: you can shop at any time, compare prices and avoid travelling.
> Dangers: some sellers are dishonest; the goods may be different from the photo; never share your PIN or password.
> Writing good sentences: one idea per sentence, a capital letter and a full stop, linking words (first, then, but, because, so).

**Card c20-1** (draft)

> Online shopping: choose, put in the cart, order, pay, then receive the delivery.
>
> _I put a pair of shoes in the cart._  
> _The delivery came two days later._  

Check question: `e2-20-steps` · ordering, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Put the steps of online shopping in order.

Shown as: Put what you want in the cart · Look at the products and prices · Order and pay · Choose a shop or an app · Receive the delivery
Correct order: Choose a shop or an app → Look at the products and prices → Put what you want in the cart → Order and pay → Receive the delivery

Full working after a second miss:

> The correct order is: Choose a shop or an app → Look at the products and prices → Put what you want in the cart → Order and pay → Receive the delivery.  

**Card c20-2** (draft)

> Shopping online has advantages and dangers. Never share your PIN or password.
>
> _Advantage: you can compare prices at home._  
> _Danger: some sellers are dishonest._

- ◻️ You can shop at night.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is an advantage._
- ✅ The goods may be different from the photo.

  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is an advantage._

Full working after a second miss:

> Goods that do not match the photo are a danger.  
> So the answer is “The goods may be different from the photo.”.  

**Card c20-3** (draft)

1. Say what you do first: First, I chose a school bag on a shopping app and compared the prices.
2. Then: Then I ordered it and paid by mobile money.
3. Add an advantage or a warning with because or but: It was easy because I did not travel, but I checked that the seller was honest.

**Answer:** First, I chose a school bag on a shopping app. Then I ordered it and paid by mobile money. It was easy because I did not travel.

## Lesson 21: Speaking and Reading- Picture talk about reaching out to people in the community and reading an interview about a doctor and a patient

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e2-21 (Humanitarian and community services; reading an interview)

**Note** (draft)

> Humanitarian work helps people who suffer, for example after a flood, a fire or a war, or people who are poor or sick. Community service is work we do for free for the people around us: cleaning the market, visiting the sick, planting trees.
> Picture talk: describe what people are doing (“A nurse is giving an injection”), then say why it helps.
> In the interview, Dr Nkeng works in a village health centre every Saturday. With volunteers, nurses, students and the Red Cross, he gives free check-ups and vaccinations. Mama Ndum could not pay, but he treated her leg, and she was grateful.
> Useful phrases: “I would like to help.” “Can I volunteer?” “Thank you for your help.”

**Card c21-1** (draft)

> Read the interview at a village health centre.
>
> _Reporter: Doctor, why do you work in the village health centre every Saturday?_  
> _Dr Nkeng: Many people here cannot travel to town. Volunteers and I give free check-ups and vaccinations._  
> _Reporter: Who helps you?_  
> _Dr Nkeng: Nurses, students and the Red Cross. Some families donate food for the patients._  
> _Reporter: Mama Ndum, what did the doctor do for you?_  
> _Mama Ndum: He treated my leg after my fall. I could not pay, but he helped me anyway. I am very grateful._

- ◻️ only at night  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: He said every Saturday._
- ◻️ every day  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: He said every Saturday._

Full working after a second miss:

> He works there every Saturday.  
> So the answer is “every Saturday”.

> Humanitarian work helps people who suffer; community service is free work for the people around us.
>

Full working after a second miss:

## Lesson 23: Vocabulary-Words related to humanitarian concerns

Prerequisites: 21 · Skill: e2-23 (Words about humanitarian concerns)

**Note** (draft)

> Humanitarian words:
> • a volunteer: a person who works for free to help;   • a donation: a gift of money, food or clothes; to donate;
> • a refugee: a person who has left their country to escape war or danger;   • a displaced person: someone forced to leave their home but still in their country;
> • shelter: a safe place to stay;   • relief: help such as food, water and medicine after a disaster;
> • a disaster: a sudden event that causes great damage (a flood, a fire, a landslide);
> • first aid: the first help given to a sick or injured person;   • a charity or an NGO: an organisation that helps people, for example the Red Cross.
> Qualities of helpers: kind, generous, caring, patient, honest.

**Card c23-1** (draft)

> A volunteer works for free; a donation is a gift to help people.
>
> _Volunteers cleaned the streets after the storm._  
> _The church collected donations of rice and soap._

- first aid → **the first help given to an injured person**
- volunteer → **a person who works for free to help**
- donation → **a gift of money, food or clothes**
- relief → **help such as food, water and medicine after a disaster**

> The right pairs are:

> donation → a gift of money, food or clothes  
> relief → help such as food, water and medicine after a disaster

## Lesson 24: Grammar- The past perfect tense

Prerequisites: 11, 15 · Skill: e2-24 (The Past Perfect tense)

**Note** (draft)

> We use the Past Perfect for an action that happened before another action in the past.
> Form: had + the past participle, the same for every person: I had finished, she had left, they had eaten.
> “When the ambulance arrived, the nurse had already stopped the bleeding.” (First the nurse stopped the bleeding; then the ambulance arrived.)
> Signal words: already, before, after, by the time, when.
> Negative: hadn't + participle: “We hadn't eaten.” Question: “Had you finished?” — “Yes, I had.”
> The later action is in the Simple Past: “By the time we got there, the meeting had begun.”

**Card c24-1** (draft)

> The Past Perfect is had + the past participle, for every person.
>
> _I had eaten lunch._  
> _They had eaten lunch._  

Check question: `e2-24-form` · fill the blank, level 1 · computed by rule, reused from practice

> Write the verb in the Past Perfect.

Sentence: **When we got to the hall, they ___ the meeting. (begin)**
Typed answer accepted (case and extra spaces ignored): **had begun**
- Typed **began** (past) → “This action came first: had + the past participle, had begun.”
- Typed **had began** (had-past) → “After had, use the past participle: begun.”
- Typed **have begun** (have) → “It is in the past: had, not have.”

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing words are “had begun”.  
> When we got to the hall, they had begun the meeting. (begin)  

**Card c24-2** (draft)

> The Past Perfect shows which past action came first.
>
> _When we arrived, the film had started. (The film started first.)_  
> _When we arrived, the film started. (We arrived first.)_  

Check question: `e2-24-first` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> “By the time the doctor came, the nurse had given first aid.” Which action happened first?

- ✅ The nurse gave first aid.
- ◻️ Both at the same time.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: By the time shows an order._
- ◻️ The doctor gave first aid.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The nurse gave it._
- ◻️ The doctor came.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: had given is the earlier action._

Full working after a second miss:

> had given came first.  
> So the answer is “The nurse gave first aid.”.  

**Card c24-3** (draft)

> By the time, already and before often go with the Past Perfect.
>
> _By the time the lorries came, the families had left._  
> _She had already eaten before the visitors arrived._  

Check question: `e2-24-c3` · fill the blank, level 1 · **authored key**

> Choose the form that fits.

Sentence: **She ___ already cooked when her husband came home.**
- ✅ had
- ◻️ has  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Earlier past action: had._
- ◻️ have  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Earlier past action: had._

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing word is “had”.  
> She had already cooked when her husband came home.  

**Worked example** (draft)

> Join the two actions in one sentence: First the nurse stopped the bleeding. Then the ambulance arrived.

1. The earlier action goes in the Past Perfect: had + stopped.
2. The later action stays in the Simple Past: the ambulance arrived.
3. When the ambulance arrived, the nurse had already stopped the bleeding.

**Answer:** When the ambulance arrived, the nurse had already stopped the bleeding.

  _↳ explanation: Use the participle: hadn't gone._
- ◻️ By the time help came, the river had flooded the road.
- ◻️ We hadn't finished our work.
- ◻️ When we arrived, the film had started.

Full working after a second miss:

> The wrong statement is “We hadn't went home.”.  
> Use the participle: hadn't gone.  

## Lesson 25: Speaking and Listening- Talk about living together in peace in the community and its advantages then listen to a passage on peaceful coexistence

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e2-25 (Living together in peace)

**Note** (draft)

> Peaceful coexistence means people who are different (in religion, language, tribe or culture) living together without violence and with respect.
> Its advantages: people feel safe, children go to school, trade grows, and the community works together.
> In the talk, Christians and Muslims, Anglophones and Francophones share the same market, school and water point. When two families quarrelled about a fence, the quarter head helped them talk; they apologised and shared the cost.
> Useful phrases:
> • Disagreeing politely: “I see your point, but …” “I'm afraid I don't agree.”
> • Apologising: “I'm sorry. It was my fault.” Accepting: “That's all right.”
> • Solving a problem: “Let's talk about it calmly.” “Why don't we share it?”

**Card c25-1** (draft)

> Read this short talk. (In class you would listen to it.)
>
> _In our quarter live Christians and Muslims, Anglophones and Francophones, and people from many tribes. We speak different languages at home, but we share the same market, school and water point._  
> _Living together in peace means respecting other people's beliefs and customs, even when they are different from ours. When we disagree, we talk calmly and listen to each other._  
> _Last year two families quarrelled about a fence. The quarter head invited them to talk. Each family explained, they apologised to each other, and they agreed to share the cost of a new fence._  

Check question: `e2-25-c1` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**

> From the talk: What do the people of the quarter share?

- ◻️ the same religion  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: They are Christians and Muslims._
- ◻️ the same tribe  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: They come from many tribes._
- ✅ the same market, school and water point
- ◻️ the same language at home  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: They speak different languages at home._

Full working after a second miss:

> The talk says they share the market, school and water point.  
> So the answer is “the same market, school and water point”.  

**Card c25-2** (draft)

> Disagree politely: say you understand, then give your view.
>
> _“I see your point, but I think we should wait.”_  
> _“I'm afraid I don't agree.”_  

Check question: `e2-25-say` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which is the most polite way to disagree?

- ◻️ That is rubbish.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is rude._
- ◻️ Shut up!  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is rude._
- ◻️ You are stupid.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That insults the person._
- ✅ I see your point, but I don't agree.

Full working after a second miss:

> Say you understand, then give your view.  
> So the answer is “I see your point, but I don't agree.”.  

**Card c25-3** (draft)

> Apologise when you are wrong, and accept an apology kindly.
>
> _“I'm sorry. It was my fault.”_  
> _“That's all right. Let's forget it.”_  

Check question: `e2-25-reply` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

  _↳ feedback if chosen: That causes a new problem._

  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is unkind._

Full working after a second miss:

> Accept the apology kindly.

**Worked example** (draft)

> Ayuk and Bih both want the same seat in class. Write what they can say to solve the problem peacefully.

1. Ayuk disagrees politely: “I see your point, but I sat here first.”
2. Bih suggests a solution: “Why don't we take turns? You today, me tomorrow.”
3. They agree and apologise: “That's fair. Sorry I shouted.” — “That's all right.”

**Answer:** They speak calmly, disagree politely, suggest taking turns and apologise.

## Lesson 26: Vocabulary- Synonyms

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e2-26 (Synonyms)

**Note** (draft)

> Synonyms are words with the same or nearly the same meaning: big and large, start and begin, quick and fast, happy and glad.
> We use synonyms so as not to repeat the same word, and to choose the best word for the situation.
> Some synonyms are more formal: buy → purchase, help → assist, enough → sufficient, end → finish or conclude.
> Synonyms are not always used in the same way: we say “a big brother”, not “a large brother”; “fast food”, not “quick food”.
> Antonyms are opposites: big and small, start and finish. Do not mix them up with synonyms.

**Card c26-1** (draft)

> Synonyms have the same or nearly the same meaning.
>
> _big = large; start = begin_  
> _happy = glad; quick = fast_  

Check question: `e2-26-match` · matching, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Match each word to its synonym.

Right-hand side (shuffled): select · sufficient · large · clever · assist
- help → **assist**
- big → **large**
- enough → **sufficient**
- smart → **clever**
- choose → **select**

Full working after a second miss:

> The right pairs are:  
> help → assist  
> big → large  
> enough → sufficient  
> smart → clever  
> choose → select  

**Card c26-2** (draft)

> Some synonyms are more formal, for letters and speeches.
>
> _buy → purchase; help → assist_  
> _enough → sufficient_  

Check question: `e2-26-pick` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

- ◻️ watch  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Watch is only looking._
- ◻️ stop  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Stop is not help._
- ✅ help
- ◻️ insult  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Insult is unkind._

Full working after a second miss:

> Assist means help.  
> So the answer is “help”.  

**Card c26-3** (draft)

> Antonyms are opposites. Do not confuse them with synonyms.
>
> _Synonyms: big, large. Antonyms: big, small._  
> _Synonyms: start, begin. Antonyms: start, finish._  

Check question: `e2-26-c3` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**

  _↳ feedback if chosen: Those are opposites._

  _↳ feedback if chosen: Those are opposites._
- ✅ quick – fast
- ◻️ up – down  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Those are opposites._

Full working after a second miss:

> Quick and fast mean the same.  
> So the answer is “quick – fast”.

**Answer:** The big market has a large hall, and it begins early.

## Lesson 27: Grammar- Regular and irregular verbs

Prerequisites: 11, 15 · Skill: e2-27 (Regular and irregular verbs)

**Note** (draft)

> Every verb has three main forms: the base (go), the simple past (went) and the past participle (gone), used after have, has and had.
> Regular verbs add -ed for both: visit, visited, visited. Spelling: stop → stopped, carry → carried, arrive → arrived, travel → travelled.
> Irregular verbs change in their own way, and must be learnt:
> • all three the same: put, put, put; cut, cut, cut;
> • past = participle: buy, bought, bought; bring, brought, brought; leave, left, left;
> • all three different: go, went, gone; see, saw, seen; write, wrote, written; begin, began, begun; speak, spoke, spoken.
> Use the simple past alone (I saw), and the participle after have, has or had (I have seen, she had gone).

**Card c27-1** (draft)

> Regular verbs add -ed for the simple past and the past participle.
>
> _visit, visited, visited_  
> _carry, carried, carried_  

Check question: `e2-27-regular` · fill the blank, level 1 · computed by rule, reused from practice

> Write the simple past.

Sentence: **clean → ___**
Typed answer accepted (case and extra spaces ignored): **cleaned**

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing word is “cleaned”.  
> clean → cleaned  

**Card c27-2** (draft)

> Irregular verbs have their own forms. Learn them in groups.
>
> _buy, bought, bought (past = participle)_  
> _go, went, gone (all different)_  

Check question: `e2-27-past` · fill the blank, level 1 · computed by rule, reused from practice

> Write the simple past.

Sentence: **begin → ___**
Typed answer accepted (case and extra spaces ignored): **began**
- Typed **begined** (ed) → “begin is irregular: began.”
- Typed **begun** (participle) → “That is the participle; the simple past is began.”

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing word is “began”.  
> begin → began  

**Card c27-3** (draft)

> Use the participle after have, has or had; use the simple past alone.
>
> _I saw the film yesterday._  
> _I have seen the film._

Typed answer accepted (case and extra spaces ignored): **taken**

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing word is “taken”.  
> I have taken (take)

>
> _regular: play, played_  
> _irregular: speak, spoke, spoken_

## Lesson 28: Writing- Write about cultural diversity and tolerance

Prerequisites: 25 · Skill: e2-28 (Writing about cultural diversity and tolerance)

**Note** (draft)

> Cultural diversity means many different cultures living side by side: languages, religions, foods, dances, dress and festivals. Cameroon has more than two hundred ethnic groups, two official languages (English and French) and many religions.
> Tolerance means accepting people whose ideas, beliefs or customs are different from ours, and treating them with respect.
> Writing a short composition:
> • Introduction: say what cultural diversity is.
> • Body: give examples, then explain why tolerance matters (peace, friendship, learning from each other).
> • Conclusion: say what we can do (learn a greeting in another language, share food, invite friends to our festivals).
> Use linking words: for example, also, however, because, so, in conclusion.

**Card c28-1** (draft)

> Cultural diversity means many cultures together; tolerance means accepting and respecting differences.
>
> _Diversity: many languages, foods and festivals._  
> _Tolerance: I respect my friend's religion even if it is not mine._  

Check question: `e2-28-c1` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**

> Which is a good way to show tolerance?

- ◻️ Refuse to sit with them.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That excludes them._
- ✅ Learn a greeting in a classmate's language.
- ◻️ Tell them to change religion.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That does not respect their beliefs._
- ◻️ Make fun of their accent.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is mocking._

Full working after a second miss:

> Learning their greeting shows respect.  
> So the answer is “Learn a greeting in a classmate's language.”.  

**Card c28-2** (draft)

> A composition has an introduction, a body and a conclusion.
>
> _Introduction: what the topic is._  
> _Body: examples and reasons. Conclusion: what we should do._  

Check question: `e2-28-parts` · ordering, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Put the parts of the composition in order.

Shown as: Tolerance is important because it helps us live in peace · For example, in my class we speak Bakweri, Bamileke, Fulfulde and Pidgin · In conclusion, we should respect and learn from each other · Cameroon has many cultures, languages and religions
Correct order: Cameroon has many cultures, languages and religions → For example, in my class we speak Bakweri, Bamileke, Fulfulde and Pidgin → Tolerance is important because it helps us live in peace → In conclusion, we should respect and learn from each other

Full working after a second miss:

> The correct order is: Cameroon has many cultures, languages and religions → For example, in my class we speak Bakweri, Bamileke, Fulfulde and Pidgin → Tolerance is important because it helps us live in peace → In conclusion, we should respect and learn from each other.  

**Card c28-3** (draft)

> Linking words join ideas: for example, also, however, because, so, in conclusion.
>
> _We eat different foods, for example achu and ndolé._  
> _In conclusion, tolerance keeps the peace._  

Check question: `e2-28-link` · fill the blank, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Choose the linking word that fits.

Sentence: **___, diversity makes our country rich.**
- ✅ In conclusion
- ◻️ Because  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Because needs a reason after it._
- ◻️ For example  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: This sums up: in conclusion._

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing words are “In conclusion”.  
> In conclusion, diversity makes our country rich.  

**Worked example** (draft)

> Plan three short paragraphs on cultural diversity in my school.

1. Introduction: In my school there are pupils from many tribes, who speak different languages and follow different religions.
2. Body: For example, we eat achu, ndolé and koki at the school fair. Tolerance matters because it keeps us friends.
3. Conclusion: In conclusion, we should learn from each other, for example by learning greetings in other languages.

**Answer:** Introduction (what diversity is) → body (examples and why tolerance matters) → conclusion (what we can do).

**Paper task** (draft; not marked by the app, mastery stays unknown)

> Write a short composition of three paragraphs (about one hundred and twenty words) on the topic “Cultural diversity and tolerance in my community”. Use an introduction, a body with examples, and a conclusion, and at least three linking words.

Model answer, shown after the pupil has written:

> My community is like a small Cameroon. People from many tribes live here: Bakweri, Bamileke, Bassa, Fulani and others. We speak different languages and follow different religions.  
> For example, at the end of the year our school holds a cultural day. Pupils wear their traditional dress, sing and share food such as achu, ndolé and kilishi. Tolerance is important because it helps us to live in peace. However, some people still laugh at other people's accents, and this hurts.  
> In conclusion, diversity makes us rich. We should respect each other's beliefs, learn new greetings and invite our friends to our festivals.  

Self-check list:

- [ ] My composition has three paragraphs: introduction, body, conclusion.
- [ ] I explained what cultural diversity is.
- [ ] I gave at least two examples from my community.
- [ ] I explained why tolerance matters.
- [ ] I used at least three linking words.
- [ ] I did not insult or make fun of any group.
- [ ] I checked my spelling and punctuation.
