## Lesson 29: Speaking and Reading- Talk about special days, national days and holidays and read a dialogue about national public holidays

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e2-29 (Special days, national days and public holidays)

**Note** (draft)

> Public holidays in Cameroon include New Year's Day (1 January), Youth Day (11 February), Labour Day (1 May), National Day (20 May), Assumption Day (15 August) and Christmas Day (25 December), and religious holidays whose dates change: Good Friday, Ascension Day, Eid al-Fitr (the end of Ramadan) and Eid al-Adha (the Feast of the Ram).
> On National Day we celebrate the unity of Cameroon; on Youth Day young people march; on Labour Day workers march.
> Special family days: birthdays, weddings, naming ceremonies.
> Talking about holidays: “What do you do on National Day?” — “I march with my school.” “Happy New Year!” “Merry Christmas!” “Happy birthday!”
> Names of holidays begin with capital letters.

**Card c29-1** (draft)

> Read the dialogue about National Day.
>
> _Tabe: Why is there no school on Thursday?_  
> _Manka: It's the twentieth of May, National Day. We celebrate the unity of Cameroon._  
> _Tabe: Are you going to march?_  
> _Manka: Yes, our school will march past the grandstand. Then my family will have a big lunch._  
> _Tabe: I marched on Youth Day in February. Which holiday do you like best?_  
> _Manka: Christmas! But I also enjoy the Feast of the Ram with my Muslim neighbours._  

Check question: `e2-29-c1` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**

> From the dialogue: Which holiday does Manka like best?

- ◻️ Youth Day  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Tabe marched on Youth Day._
- ◻️ National Day  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: She talks about it, but likes Christmas best._
- ◻️ Labour Day  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: She does not mention it._
- ✅ Christmas

Full working after a second miss:

> Manka says: Christmas!  
> So the answer is “Christmas”.  

**Card c29-2** (draft)

> Each national day has its own date and meaning.
>
> _National Day, the 20 of May: the unity of Cameroon._  
> _Youth Day, the 11 of February: young people march._

- Youth Day → **11 February**

> The right pairs are:

> New Year's Day → 1 January  
> Labour Day → 1 May

## Lesson 30: Vocabulary-Word formation

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e2-30 (Word formation: prefixes and suffixes)

**Note** (draft)

> We can make new words by adding a part at the beginning (a prefix) or at the end (a suffix).
> Prefixes change the meaning: un- (unhappy), dis- (disagree), im- (impossible, before m and p), in- (incorrect), re- (rewrite: write again), mis- (misunderstand: understand wrongly).
> Suffixes change the kind of word:
> • verb → noun: celebrate → celebration, govern → government, decorate → decoration, enjoy → enjoyment, sing → singer;
> • adjective → noun: happy → happiness, kind → kindness, free → freedom;
> • noun → adjective: care → careful (with care) or careless (without care), peace → peaceful, joy → joyful, nation → national;
> • adjective → adverb: quick → quickly, happy → happily.
> Spelling: y after a consonant becomes i (happy → happiness), and a final e often drops before -ion (celebrate → celebration).

**Card c30-1** (draft)

> A prefix goes at the beginning and changes the meaning: un-, dis-, im-, re-, mis-.
>
> _happy → unhappy; agree → disagree_  
> _possible → impossible; write → rewrite_  

Check question: `e2-30-prefix` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which word means “not agree”?

- ◻️ misagree  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Mis- means wrongly._
- ◻️ imagree  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Im- goes before m and p._
- ✅ disagree
- ◻️ unagree  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Agree takes dis-._

Full working after a second miss:

> dis- + agree = disagree.  
> So the answer is “disagree”.  

**Card c30-2** (draft)

> Suffixes -ion, -ment and -ness make nouns.
>
> _celebrate → celebration; govern → government_  
> _kind → kindness; happy → happiness_

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing word is “freedom”.  
> We fought for our freedom. (free)  

**Card c30-3** (draft)

> Suffixes -ful, -less and -al make adjectives; -ly makes adverbs.
>
> _care → careful, careless; nation → national_  
> _quick → quickly_

**Answer:** celebration, peaceful, kindness

## Lesson 31: Grammar-Modals and polite expression

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e2-31 (Modals and polite expressions)

**Note** (draft)

> Modal verbs go before a base verb and add a meaning:
> • can: ability or permission — “I can swim.” “Can I go out?”
> • could: a more polite request, or past ability — “Could you help me, please?” “I could read at five.”
> • may: polite permission or possibility — “May I come in?” “It may rain.”
> • must: strong obligation — “You must wear a uniform.” mustn't: it is forbidden.
> • should: advice — “You should rest.”
> • would: polite offers and requests — “Would you like some tea?” “Would you open the window, please?”
> After a modal, use the base verb with no to and no -s: “She can swim” (not “she cans” or “can to swim”).
> Polite expressions: please, thank you, excuse me, I'm sorry, “Would you mind …?”

**Card c31-1** (draft)

> After a modal (can, could, may, must, should, would), use the base verb: no to, no -s.
>
> _She can sing. (not: cans sing, can to sing)_  
> _You should rest._  

Check question: `e2-31-form` · fill the blank, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Choose the correct form.

Sentence: **My sister can ___ very well.**
- ✅ sing
- ◻️ sings  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: After can, no -s: sing._
- ◻️ to sing  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: After can, no to: sing._

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing word is “sing”.  
> My sister can sing very well.  

**Card c31-2** (draft)

> Each modal has its own meaning: must for obligation, should for advice, may for permission.
>
> _You must wear a uniform. (rule)_  
> _You should drink water. (advice)_

- ◻️ should  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Should is advice._

Full working after a second miss:

> Past ability: could.  
> So the answer is “could”.  

**Card c31-3** (draft)

> Could, may and would make requests and offers more polite.
>
> _Could you help me, please?_  
> _Would you like some tea?_

Tiles: in · I · May · come (then “?”)
Answer: **May I come in?**

Full working after a second miss:

> The sentence is: May I come in?

- ◻️ You should drink more water.
- ✅ You should to see a doctor.  
  _↳ explanation: No to after should._

Full working after a second miss:

> The wrong statement is “You should to see a doctor.”.  
> No to after should.  

> One sentence has a mistake. Which one?

> After must, use the base verb: must go.  

## Lesson 32: Writing-Write in two paragraphs about how young people should work to improve life in their community

Prerequisites: 28, 31 · Skill: e2-32 (Writing: how young people can improve their community)

**Note** (draft)

> Writing two paragraphs:
> • Paragraph one: the problems in the community and why young people should help (for example, dirty streets, blocked gutters, children who cannot read, lonely old people).
> • Paragraph two: what young people can do, with examples (clean-up campaigns, planting trees, helping younger children with homework, visiting the sick, avoiding drugs and violence).
> Each paragraph has a topic sentence and supporting sentences.
> Use should and can for suggestions: “Young people should …” “We can …”
> Linking words: first, also, in addition, for example, because, finally.
> End with a strong closing sentence.

**Card c32-1** (draft)

> Each paragraph starts with a topic sentence; the other sentences support it.
>
> _Topic: “Young people can make our town cleaner.”_  
> _Support: “For example, we can clean the gutters every month.”_  

Check question: `e2-32-c1` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**

> Which sentence is the best topic sentence for a paragraph about what young people can do?

- ◻️ For example, we can plant trees.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is a supporting example._
- ◻️ The gutters are blocked.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is a problem, not the topic of this paragraph._
- ✅ Young people can do a lot to improve our community.
- ◻️ Finally, we went home.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is not about the topic._

Full working after a second miss:

> It states the main idea of the paragraph.  
> So the answer is “Young people can do a lot to improve our community.”.  

**Card c32-2** (draft)

> Use should and can to make suggestions.
>
> _Young people should avoid drugs._  
> _We can help younger children with their homework._

  _↳ feedback if chosen: No to after should._
- ◻️ shoulds  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Modals never take -s._
- ✅ should

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing word is “should”.  
> Young people should respect their elders.  

**Card c32-3** (draft)

> Linking words make the paragraph flow: first, also, in addition, for example, finally.
>
> _First, we can clean the market._  
> _In addition, we can plant trees along the road._

Sentence: **___, if we work together, our community will be better.**
- ✅ Finally

**Worked example** (draft)

> Write the topic sentences of the two paragraphs about young people in Kumba.

1. Paragraph one (problems): Our quarter in Kumba has dirty gutters and many children who need help with school.
2. Paragraph two (actions): Young people can do a lot to make Kumba a better place.
3. Each topic sentence is followed by examples and reasons, with should, can and linking words.

**Answer:** Problems and why we should help → what young people can do, with examples.

**Paper task** (draft; not marked by the app, mastery stays unknown)

> Write two paragraphs in your exercise book on the topic “How young people can improve life in their community”. In the first paragraph describe the problems; in the second, say what young people can do, with examples. Use should or can, and at least three linking words.

Model answer, shown after the pupil has written:

> Our community has many problems. The gutters are often blocked with rubbish, so the streets flood when it rains. Some children cannot read well because nobody helps them at home, and some old people live alone. Young people should help, because this is our home and our future.  
> There are many things young people can do. First, we can organise a clean-up campaign every month to clear the gutters and the market. In addition, older pupils can teach younger children to read after school. We can also visit old people and fetch water for them. Finally, we should avoid drugs and violence and be good examples. If we work together, our community will be cleaner, safer and happier.  

Self-check list:

- [ ] I wrote two paragraphs.
- [ ] Each paragraph starts with a topic sentence.
- [ ] Paragraph one describes problems; paragraph two gives actions with examples.
- [ ] I used should or can.
- [ ] I used at least three linking words.
- [ ] I ended with a strong closing sentence.
- [ ] I checked capital letters, full stops and spelling.

## Lesson 33: Speaking and Reading- Talk about insecurity and crime in the community and read a text on criminality, terror and kidnapping

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e2-33 (Talking about insecurity and crime; reading about a safer quarter)

**Note** (draft)

> Text: A safer quarter
> Last year, Mbengwi quarter lived in fear. Thieves broke into shops at night, and two pupils were nearly kidnapped on their way home. Parents stopped sending children to evening studies. Then the quarter head called a meeting. The people agreed to light the streets, to form a vigilance group of volunteers who walk round the quarter at night, and to report every suspect to the police instead of beating him. The police opened a small post near the market. Six months later, crime had fallen sharply. "Security is everybody's business," said the quarter head. "We protect each other, and we let the law do its work."
> 
> Useful words: theft, burglary (breaking into a house or shop), kidnapping (taking a person by force), a suspect (a person thought to be guilty), a vigilance group (volunteers who keep watch), to report a crime, the police, justice.

## Lesson 34: Vocabulary-Idiomatic expressions

Prerequisites: 33 · Skill: e2-34 (Idiomatic expressions)

**Note** (draft)

> An idiom is a group of words whose meaning is different from the meanings of the separate words. “He is behind bars” does not mean he is standing behind some bars: it means he is in prison.
> • caught red-handed: caught while doing something wrong
> • behind bars: in prison
> • on the run: running away from the police
> • take the law into your own hands: punish someone yourself instead of going to the police
> • keep an eye on: watch carefully
> • turn a blind eye: pretend not to see something wrong
> • break the law: do something illegal
> • cry wolf: give a false alarm
> Learn idioms as whole expressions, and use them in the right situation.

**Card c34-1** (draft)

> An idiom means more than its words.
>
> _“Behind bars” means in prison._  

Check question: `e2-34-mean` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> What does “keep an eye on” mean?

- ✅ watch carefully
- ◻️ caught while doing something wrong  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is the meaning of another idiom._
- ◻️ in prison  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is the meaning of another idiom._
- ◻️ running away from the police  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is the meaning of another idiom._

Full working after a second miss:

> “keep an eye on” means watch carefully.  
> So the answer is “watch carefully”.  

**Card c34-2** (draft)

> Choose the idiom that fits the situation.
>
> _Do not take the law into your own hands: call the police._

Sentence: **After the trial, the kidnapper spent ten years ___.**

- ◻️ red-handed  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: “Red-handed” is about being caught in the act._

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing words are “behind bars”.

> Some idioms warn or advise.
>
> _Keep an eye on your bag in the market._

## Lesson 35: Grammar- Auxiliary verbs

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e2-35 (Auxiliary verbs)

**Note** (draft)

> Auxiliary (helping) verbs come before a main verb.
> • Primary auxiliaries: be (am, is, are, was, were) for continuous tenses — She is reading; have (has, have, had) for perfect tenses — They have finished; do (do, does, did) for questions and negatives in simple tenses — Does he play? He did not come.
> • Modal auxiliaries: can, could, may, might, must, shall, should, will, would. They are followed by the bare infinitive: You must report it (not “must to report”).
> The auxiliary agrees with the subject: he does / they do; she has / we have; I am / he is / they are.

**Card c35-1** (draft)

> Do / does / did make questions and negatives in simple tenses.
>
> _Does she live here? He did not see the thief._  

Check question: `e2-35-do` · fill the blank, level 1 · computed by rule, reused from practice

> Choose the auxiliary.

Sentence: **___ my cousins live near the market?**
- ◻️ Does  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: my cousins goes with do._
- ◻️ Are  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: “live” is a main verb in the simple present: the question uses do or does._
- ✅ Do

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing word is “Do”.  
> Do my cousins live near the market?  

**Card c35-2** (draft)

> Be + -ing for continuous tenses; have + past participle for perfect tenses.
>
> _They are watching. She has called the police._  

Check question: `e2-35-be` · fill the blank, level 1 · computed by rule, reused from practice

> Choose the auxiliary.

Sentence: **Tabe and Ndi ___ waiting for the police now.**
- ◻️ do  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The continuous tense uses be + -ing, not do._
- ✅ are
- ◻️ is  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Tabe and Ndi goes with are._

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing word is “are”.  
> Tabe and Ndi are waiting for the police now.  

**Card c35-3** (draft)

> Modals take the bare infinitive.
>
> _You must report it. We should help._

Sentence: **We should ___ our neighbours.**

- ✅ help

> We should help our neighbours.

1. Simple present: use do or does: Do the police come at night?
2. Present perfect: move has before the subject: Has Ewane finished?

## Lesson 36: Writing-Informal letter to a friend

Prerequisites: 33 · Skill: e2-36 (Writing an informal letter to a friend)

**Note** (draft)

> An informal letter is written to a friend or a relative, in a friendly style.
> Layout: your address and the date at the top right; “Dear + first name,” on the left; an opening paragraph (greetings, why you write); one or two body paragraphs (your news); a closing paragraph (best wishes, ask for a reply); a friendly ending (Your friend, / With love,) and your first name.
> Use everyday language and contractions (I'm, don't), but write correct sentences.

**Card c36-1** (draft)

> The address and the date go at the top right.
>
> _P.O. Box …, Bamenda — 12 March_

Correct order: Box 214, Bamenda, 12 March → Dear Tabe, → I hope you are well. I am writing to tell you good news. → Our quarter is now safe because everybody helps. → Write back soon. → Your friend, Ngum

**Worked example** (draft)

> Plan a letter to your friend about the meeting that made your quarter safer.

1. Top right: your address and the date.
2. Dear Tabe, — I hope you are well. I am writing to tell you good news from our quarter.
3. Body: the problem (thefts), the meeting, what people agreed to do, the result.
4. Closing: Greet your family for me and write back soon. Your friend, Ngum

**Answer:** Address and date → Dear … → opening → news → closing → Your friend, + name

**Paper task** (draft; not marked by the app, mastery stays unknown)

> Write an informal letter (about 150 words) in your exercise book to a friend in another town. Tell him or her how people in your quarter fight insecurity. Use the layout of an informal letter.

Model answer, shown after the pupil has written:

> Box 214, Bamenda
12 March

Dear Tabe,

I hope you and your family are well. I am writing to tell you some good news from our quarter.

Last year thieves broke into shops almost every week, and we were afraid to go out at night. Then our quarter head called a meeting. We agreed to put lights in the streets and to form a vigilance group. My father and other volunteers now walk round the quarter at night, and they call the police when they see a suspect. Nobody beats suspects any more: we let the law do its work.

Now the market is busy again in the evening and I go to evening studies without fear.

Greet your parents for me and write back soon.

Your friend,
Ngum  

Self-check list:

- [ ] My address and the date are at the top right.
- [ ] I began with “Dear + first name,”.
- [ ] My opening paragraph greets my friend and says why I am writing.
- [ ] My body paragraphs give my news in order.
- [ ] I closed with good wishes and asked for a reply.
- [ ] I ended with “Your friend,” and my first name.
- [ ] My sentences start with capitals and end with full stops.

## Lesson 37: Speaking and Reading -Talk about rights in general and read a text on young parliamentarians advocating children's rights

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e2-37 (Talking about rights; reading about young parliamentarians)

**Note** (draft)

> Text: The Children's Parliament
> Every year, children chosen from schools all over Cameroon meet as members of the Children's Parliament. Like real members of parliament, they debate and vote. This year, Junior Parliamentarian Ayuk Mary spoke about the right to education: "Some girls stop school because they must work at home or marry too young. Every child has the right to go to school, to be healthy, to be protected from violence and to be heard." Another young parliamentarian reminded the house that rights come with duties: "We must respect our parents and teachers, study hard and protect the environment." At the end, the children sent their proposals to the Minister.
> 
> A right is something every person should have or be allowed to do (to go to school, to be protected). A duty is something we must do (to respect others, to obey the law). 

**Card c37-1** (draft)

> A right is what a child should have; a duty is what a child must do.
>
> _Right: to go to school. Duty: to study hard._  

Check question: `e2-37-right` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Is this a right or a duty? “to protect the environment”

- ◻️ a crime  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: It is not a crime._
- ◻️ a right  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: It is something we must do._
- ✅ a duty
- ◻️ a punishment  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: It is not a punishment._

Full working after a second miss:

> Protecting the environment is a duty.  
> So the answer is “a duty”.  

**Card c37-2** (draft)

> Find the answer in the text and quote it.
>
> _Who spoke about education? Ayuk Mary._  

Check question: `e2-37-text` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Text “The Children's Parliament”: who spoke about the right to education?

- ◻️ a parent  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The text does not say that._
- ◻️ the Minister  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The Minister received the proposals._
- ✅ Ayuk Mary
- ◻️ a teacher  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The text does not say that._

Full working after a second miss:

> “Junior Parliamentarian Ayuk Mary spoke about the right to education.”  
> So the answer is “Ayuk Mary”.  

**Card c37-3** (draft)

> Rights come with duties.
>
> _The right to education comes with the duty to study._  

Check question: `e2-37-idea-card` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**

> Text “The Children's Parliament”: how is the Children's Parliament like the real parliament?

- ◻️ it makes the laws of the country  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: It sends proposals; it does not make laws._
- ◻️ the members are paid  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The text does not say that._
- ✅ the members debate and vote
- ◻️ the members are adults  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The members are children._

Full working after a second miss:

> “Like real members of parliament, they debate and vote.”  
> So the answer is “the members debate and vote”.  

**Worked example** (draft)

1. Rights: “to go to school, to be healthy, to be protected from violence and to be heard”.
2. Duties: “respect our parents and teachers, study hard and protect the environment”.

**Answer:** rights: education, protection; duties: respect, studying hard

## Lesson 39: Abbreviations and acronyms

Prerequisites: 37 · Skill: e2-39 (Abbreviations and acronyms)

**Note** (draft)

> An abbreviation is a short form of a word or a group of words: Dr (Doctor), Mr (Mister), km (kilometre), e.g. (for example), i.e. (that is), etc. (and so on).
> An initialism is made of first letters, each letter said separately: UN (United Nations), WHO (World Health Organization), NGO, GCE.
> An acronym is made of first letters but read as one word: UNICEF (/ˈjuːnɪsef/), AIDS, NATO.
> Write abbreviations of organisations in capital letters; use them only after the full name has been written once in a formal text.

**Card c39-1** (draft)

> An abbreviation is a short form of words.
>
> _Dr = Doctor; km = kilometre_  

Check question: `e2-39-mean` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> What does “Dr” stand for?

- ✅ Doctor
- ◻️ World Health Organization  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is another abbreviation's meaning._
- ◻️ United Nations  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is another abbreviation's meaning._
- ◻️ United Nations Children's Fund  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is another abbreviation's meaning._

Full working after a second miss:

> Dr stands for Doctor.  
> So the answer is “Doctor”.  

**Card c39-2** (draft)

> An acronym is read as one word (UNICEF); an initialism letter by letter (UN).
>
> _UNICEF → “you-ni-sef”; WHO → “W-H-O”_  

Check question: `e2-39-kind` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which is read letter by letter?

- ◻️ UNICEF  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: UNICEF is read as a word._
- ✅ WHO
- ◻️ AIDS  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: AIDS is read as a word._
- ◻️ NATO  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: NATO is read as a word._

Full working after a second miss:

> We say W-H-O.  
> So the answer is “WHO”.  

**Card c39-3** (draft)

> e.g. introduces examples; i.e. explains.
>
> _fruits, e.g. mangoes; the capital, i.e. Yaoundé_  

Check question: `e2-39-use-card` · fill the blank, level 1 · **authored key**

> Choose the abbreviation that fits.

Sentence: **The school is 3 ___ from my house.**
- ◻️ Dr  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Dr means Doctor._
- ◻️ No.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: No. means number._
- ✅ km

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing word is “km”.  
> The school is 3 km from my house.  

**Worked example** (draft)

> What do WHO and e.g. stand for, and how are they read?

1. WHO = World Health Organization: we say each letter, W-H-O.
2. e.g. = for example (from Latin); we say “for example”.

**Answer:** World Health Organization; for example

## Lesson 40: Writing-Write about the rights and duties of a girl child

Prerequisites: 37 · Skill: e2-40 (Writing about the rights and duties of the girl child)

**Note** (draft)

> To write a short expository text: a title; an introduction that says what you will write about; one paragraph on rights and one on duties, each with a topic sentence and examples; a conclusion that gives your opinion.
> Linking words: first, also, in addition, however, finally, in conclusion.

**Card c40-1** (draft)

> Each paragraph has one main idea and a topic sentence.
>
> _A girl child has important rights._

Shown as: However, she also has duties: to respect others and to study hard. · First, a girl has the right to go to school and to be protected. · The rights and duties of the girl child · In conclusion, rights and duties go together. · Like every child, a girl has rights and duties.
Correct order: The rights and duties of the girl child → Like every child, a girl has rights and duties. → First, a girl has the right to go to school and to be protected. → However, she also has duties: to respect others and to study hard. → In conclusion, rights and duties go together.

Check question: `e2-40-order` · ordering, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Put the parts of the text in order.

Shown as: First, a girl has the right to go to school and to be protected. · However, she also has duties: to respect others and to study hard. · Like every child, a girl has rights and duties. · The rights and duties of the girl child · In conclusion, rights and duties go together.
Correct order: The rights and duties of the girl child → Like every child, a girl has rights and duties. → First, a girl has the right to go to school and to be protected. → However, she also has duties: to respect others and to study hard. → In conclusion, rights and duties go together.

Full working after a second miss:

> The correct order is: The rights and duties of the girl child → Like every child, a girl has rights and duties. → First, a girl has the right to go to school and to be protected. → However, she also has duties: to respect others and to study hard. → In conclusion, rights and duties go together..  

**Worked example** (draft)

> Plan a text on the rights and duties of the girl child.

1. Title: The rights and duties of the girl child.
2. Introduction: like every child, a girl has rights, and these rights come with duties.
3. First paragraph (rights): education, health, protection from violence and early marriage, to be heard.
4. Second paragraph (duties): respect, study, help at home, protect the environment.
5. Conclusion: when girls are protected and responsible, the whole community gains.

**Answer:** title → introduction → rights → duties → conclusion

**Paper task** (draft; not marked by the app, mastery stays unknown)

> Write a text of about 150 words in your exercise book: “The rights and duties of the girl child”. Give a title, an introduction, one paragraph on rights, one on duties and a conclusion.

Model answer, shown after the pupil has written:

> The rights and duties of the girl child

Like every child, a girl has rights, and these rights come with duties.

First, a girl has the right to go to school, just like a boy. She has the right to good health care and to be protected from violence and from early marriage. She also has the right to give her opinion on matters that concern her.

However, she also has duties. She must respect her parents, her teachers and her elders. She must study hard and help at home, and, like everybody, she must protect the environment.

In conclusion, when a girl's rights are respected and she carries out her duties, she grows into a responsible woman who helps her community.  

Self-check list:

- [ ] I wrote a title.
- [ ] My introduction says what the text is about.
- [ ] One paragraph is about rights, with examples.
- [ ] One paragraph is about duties, with examples.
- [ ] I used linking words (first, also, however, in conclusion).
- [ ] My conclusion gives my opinion.
- [ ] My sentences are correct and punctuated.

Correct order: The rights and duties of the girl child → Like every child, a girl has rights and duties. → First, a girl has the right to go to school and to be protected. → However, she also has duties: to respect others and to study hard. → In conclusion, rights and duties go together.

## Lesson 41: Listening and Reading- Accessing public health services in Cameroon

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e2-41 (Accessing public health services; reading about a visit to the health centre)

**Note** (draft)

> Text: At the health centre
> When Bih's little brother had a high fever, their mother took him to the integrated health centre near their village. First, a nurse wrote his name in the register and took his temperature and weight. Then they waited their turn to see the nurse in charge, who examined the boy. A quick test in the laboratory showed malaria. The nurse explained the treatment: tablets three times a day for three days, plenty of water, and sleeping under a treated mosquito net. Bih's mother paid at the cash desk, collected the medicine at the pharmacy and booked a check-up for the following week. On the way out, a poster reminded them that children's vaccinations are given free every Tuesday.
> 
> Steps of a visit: registration, waiting, consultation, laboratory test, payment, pharmacy, follow-up visit.

## Lesson 42: Vocabulary-Affixes

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e2-42 (Affixes: prefixes and suffixes)

**Note** (draft)

> An affix is a group of letters added to a word to make a new word.
> • A prefix goes at the beginning and often changes the meaning: un- (unhappy), dis- (dishonest), im- (impossible), in- (incorrect), re- (again: rewrite), mis- (wrongly: misunderstand), pre- (before: prepay).
> • A suffix goes at the end and often changes the class of word: -ful (careful), -less (without: hopeless), -ment (treatment), -ness (illness), -er / -ist (a person: teacher, scientist).
> Health words with affixes: unhealthy, illness, treatment, careless, painful, painless.

**Card c42-1** (draft)

> A prefix comes first and changes the meaning.
>
> _un- + happy = unhappy (not happy)_  

Check question: `e2-42-pre` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which word means “not honest”?

- ◻️ impossible  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That word comes from another root._
- ✅ dishonest
- ◻️ unhappy  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That word comes from another root._
- ◻️ incorrect  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That word comes from another root._

Full working after a second miss:

> dis- + honest = dishonest.  
> So the answer is “dishonest”.  

**Card c42-2** (draft)

> A suffix comes last and can make a new kind of word.
>
> _treat (verb) + -ment = treatment (noun)_  

Check question: `e2-42-suf` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Add a suffix to “teach” to make the word that fits: a person who teaches is a …

- ◻️ teachment  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Check the meaning of the suffix._
- ◻️ teachist  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Check the meaning of the suffix._
- ✅ teacher
- ◻️ teachness  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Check the meaning of the suffix._

Full working after a second miss:

> teach + -er = teacher.  
> So the answer is “teacher”.  

**Card c42-3** (draft)

> -ful means full of; -less means without.
>
> _painful (full of pain), painless (without pain)_

## Lesson 43: Grammar- Imperatives

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e2-43 (Imperatives)

**Note** (draft)

> The imperative gives orders, instructions, advice or warnings. It uses the bare infinitive, usually without a subject: Wash your hands. Take two tablets a day.
> Negative: Don't (Do not) + verb: Don't drink dirty water.
> Polite requests: add please or use Let's for suggestions: Please sit down. Let's go to the clinic.
> Health instructions are often imperatives: Shake well before use. Keep out of reach of children. Do not exceed the stated dose.

**Card c43-1** (draft)

> An imperative starts with the bare verb.
>
> _Drink clean water. Sleep under a net._  

Check question: `e2-43-form` · fill the blank, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

Sentence: **___ under a treated mosquito net.**
- ◻️ Slept  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The imperative is not in the past._
- ◻️ Sleeps  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: No -s in the imperative._
- ✅ Sleep

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing word is “Sleep”.  
> Sleep under a treated mosquito net.  

**Card c43-2** (draft)

> Negative imperative: Don't + verb.
>
> _Don't share needles._  

Check question: `e2-43-neg` · fill the blank, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Choose the correct negative imperative.

Sentence: **___ leave medicine where children can reach it.**

- ◻️ Didn't  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Imperatives are about now and later, not the past._
- ✅ Don't

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing word is “Don't”.  
> Don't leave medicine where children can reach it.  

**Card c43-3** (draft)

> Please makes an order polite; Let's makes a suggestion.
>
> _Please wait here. Let's clean the yard._

## Lesson 44: Writing-Write slogans on health and develop into paragraphs

Prerequisites: 41, 43 · Skill: e2-44 (Writing health slogans and developing them into paragraphs)

**Note** (draft)

> A slogan is a short, catchy sentence that is easy to remember: “Clean hands, healthy life.” “Sleep under a net, keep malaria away.”
> Good slogans are short, use imperatives or rhythm, and give one clear message.
> To develop a slogan into a paragraph: the slogan as the first sentence or title; explain why (the reason); give examples or facts; end with an imperative that tells people what to do.

**Card c44-1** (draft)

> A slogan is short, catchy and has one message.
>
> _“Clean hands, healthy life.”_

**Paper task** (draft; not marked by the app, mastery stays unknown)

> Write three health slogans in your exercise book. Then choose one and develop it into a paragraph of about 100 words: give the reason, examples, and end with an imperative.

Model answer, shown after the pupil has written:

> Slogans: “Clean hands, healthy life.” “Sleep under a net, keep malaria away.” “Clean water, healthy life.”

Clean hands, healthy life! Our hands touch many things every day: money, door handles, toilets and food. They carry germs that cause diarrhoea, cholera and coughs. Washing our hands with soap and clean water removes most of these germs. We should wash them before eating, before cooking, after the toilet and after caring for a sick person. It takes less than a minute and costs very little, but it can save a life. Wash your hands often, and teach your little brothers and sisters to do the same.

- [ ] I explained why (the reason).
- [ ] I gave examples or facts.
- [ ] I ended with an imperative.
- [ ] My sentences are correct and punctuated.

## Lesson 45: Speaking and Reading-Talk about postal services and their importance

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e2-45 (Talking about postal services; reading about sending a parcel)

**Note** (draft)

> Text: A parcel for Grandma
> Nkeng wanted to send a parcel of clothes to her grandmother in Kumbo. She went to the post office. At the counter, the clerk weighed the parcel and told her the price. Nkeng wrote the address clearly: her grandmother's name, the P.O. Box number and the town. She also wrote her own address on the back, so that the parcel could come back if it was not delivered. She paid, received a receipt with a tracking number, and bought stamps for two letters. The clerk explained that express mail is faster but costs more. A week later, Grandma phoned: the parcel had arrived safely.
> 
> Postal services: sending letters and parcels, selling stamps, renting post office boxes (P.O. Box), express mail, sending and receiving money. Words: counter, clerk, sender, addressee (who receives it), receipt, tracking number.

**Card c45-1** (draft)

> The post office sends letters and parcels, sells stamps and rents boxes.
>
> _You buy stamps at the post office counter._

Right-hand side (shuffled): a paper that shows you have paid · the person a letter is sent to · a small paper you stick on a letter to pay for sending it · the person who sends a letter or parcel
- a stamp → **a small paper you stick on a letter to pay for sending it**
- the addressee → **the person a letter is sent to**
- a receipt → **a paper that shows you have paid**
- the sender → **the person who sends a letter or parcel**

> a receipt → a paper that shows you have paid  
> the sender → the person who sends a letter or parcel

## Lesson 46: Vocabulary-Using masculine, feminine and neuter gender

Prerequisites: 45 · Skill: e2-46 (Masculine, feminine, neuter and common gender)

**Note** (draft)

> Nouns have gender:
> • masculine: male persons or animals — postman, king, uncle, bull, cock;
> • feminine: female — postwoman, queen, aunt, cow, hen;
> • neuter: things with no sex — letter, parcel, stamp, post office;
> • common: either male or female — teacher, doctor, clerk, child, parent, friend.
> Feminine forms are made with -ess (waiter → waitress, host → hostess), with another word (postman → postwoman), or are a different word (king → queen, nephew → niece).

**Card c46-1** (draft)

> Masculine: male; feminine: female.
>
> _king → queen; uncle → aunt_  

Check question: `e2-46-fem` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> What is the feminine of “postman”?

- ◻️ waitress  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is the feminine of another noun._
- ◻️ queen  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is the feminine of another noun._
- ✅ postwoman
- ◻️ actress  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is the feminine of another noun._

Full working after a second miss:

> postman → postwoman.  
> So the answer is “postwoman”.  

**Card c46-2** (draft)

> Neuter nouns name things; common nouns can be male or female.
>
> _letter: neuter; teacher: common_  

Check question: `e2-46-kind` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> What is the gender of “nephew”?

- ◻️ common  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: A nephew is only male._
- ◻️ feminine  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: A nephew is male._
- ◻️ neuter  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: A nephew is a person._
- ✅ masculine

Full working after a second miss:

> The feminine is niece.  
> So the answer is “masculine”.  

**Card c46-3** (draft)

> Many feminine forms end in -ess.
>
> _actor → actress; host → hostess_  

Check question: `e2-46-ess-card` · fill the blank, level 1 · **authored key**

> Choose the feminine noun.

Sentence: **The ___ served us rice and fish.**
- ◻️ waitwoman  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: We say waitress._
- ✅ waitress
- ◻️ waiteress  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: waiter → waitress (the e is dropped)._

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing word is “waitress”.  
> The waitress served us rice and fish.  

**Worked example** (draft)

> Give the gender of: clerk, queen, parcel, bull.

1. clerk: male or female → common.
2. queen: female → feminine.
3. parcel: a thing → neuter.
4. bull: a male animal → masculine.

**Answer:** common, feminine, neuter, masculine

- ◻️ waitress  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is the feminine of another noun._
- ◻️ actress

Full working after a second miss:

> headmaster → headmistress.  
> So the answer is “headmistress”.

- ◻️ queen  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is the feminine of another noun._

- ◻️ postwoman

> actor → actress.

## Lesson 47: Grammar- Common and proper nouns

Prerequisites: 46 · Skill: e2-47 (Common and proper nouns)

**Note** (draft)

> A common noun names any person, place or thing of a kind: town, post office, river, month, teacher. It starts with a small letter (except at the start of a sentence).
> A proper noun names one particular person, place or thing: Nkeng, Kumbo, the Wouri, January, Monday, Cameroon, CAMPOST. It always starts with a capital letter.
> Days, months, countries, towns, rivers, mountains, people's names and the names of organisations are proper nouns.

**Card c47-1** (draft)

> A proper noun names one particular person, place or thing.
>
> _Kumbo is a proper noun; town is a common noun._  

Check question: `e2-47-kind` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> In “Nkeng sent a parcel.”, which word is a proper noun?

- ◻️ parcel  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is a common noun._
- ◻️ a  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is an article._
- ✅ Nkeng
- ◻️ sent  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is a verb._

Full working after a second miss:

> Nkeng is a particular person.  
> So the answer is “Nkeng”.  

**Card c47-2** (draft)

> Proper nouns always start with a capital letter.
>
> _Monday, March, Cameroon, Mount Cameroon_  

Check question: `e2-47-cap` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which sentence is written correctly?

- ✅ The parcel went to Bamenda.
- ◻️ The Parcel went to Bamenda.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Parcel is a common noun._
- ◻️ the parcel went to Bamenda.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: A sentence starts with a capital letter._
- ◻️ The parcel went to bamenda.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Towns are proper nouns: Bamenda._

Full working after a second miss:

> Capital B for the town.  
> So the answer is “The parcel went to Bamenda.”.  

**Card c47-3** (draft)

> Common nouns start with a small letter inside a sentence.
>
> _She went to the post office in Kumbo._  

Check question: `e2-47-spot-card` · spot the error, level 1 · **authored key**

> Which sentence has a capital-letter mistake?

- ◻️ Nkeng lives in Bafoussam.
- ◻️ We crossed the Wouri bridge.
- ◻️ The post office closes on Saturday.
- ✅ The Post Office is near the Market.  
  _↳ explanation: post office and market are common nouns here: small letters._

Full working after a second miss:

> The wrong statement is “The Post Office is near the Market.”.  
> post office and market are common nouns here: small letters.  

**Worked example** (draft)

> Find the proper nouns: “Last january, nkeng sent a parcel from buea to her aunt in kumbo.”

1. Names of a month, a person and towns are proper nouns.
2. January, Nkeng, Buea, Kumbo need capital letters.

**Answer:** January, Nkeng, Buea, Kumbo

**Practice** `e2-47-kind` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**

- ◻️ We  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is a pronoun._
- ◻️ visited  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is a verb._
- ◻️ bank  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is a common noun._
- ✅ Friday

Full working after a second miss:

> Days are proper nouns.  
> So the answer is “Friday”.  

> In “Nkeng sent a parcel.”, which word is a proper noun?

- ◻️ sent  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is a verb._
- ✅ Nkeng
- ◻️ a  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is an article._
- ◻️ parcel  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is a common noun._

Full working after a second miss:

> Nkeng is a particular person.  
> So the answer is “Nkeng”.  

**Practice** `e2-47-cap` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**

Full working after a second miss:

> Capital M for the month, small b for birthday.

> Which sentence is written correctly?

- ◻️ The parcel went to bamenda.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Towns are proper nouns: Bamenda._
- ✅ The parcel went to Bamenda.
- ◻️ the parcel went to Bamenda.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: A sentence starts with a capital letter._
- ◻️ The Parcel went to Bamenda.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Parcel is a common noun._

Full working after a second miss:

> Capital B for the town.  
> So the answer is “The parcel went to Bamenda.”.

## Lesson 48: Writing- Write reports on health issues and poster delivery services

Prerequisites: 45, 47 · Skill: e2-48 (Writing a report and a poster)

**Note** (draft)

> A report gives facts about something that happened or was studied. Layout: a title; To (who receives it), From (who writes it), Date; an introduction (the purpose); the findings (what you saw or found, in paragraphs or points); recommendations (what should be done). Use formal language and the past tense for what happened.
> A poster advertises a service or gives a message. It has a short heading, a picture idea, a few key facts (what, where, when, how much), and contact details. Use few words and large letters.

**Card c48-1** (draft)

> A report gives facts: introduction, findings, recommendations.
>
> _Recommendation: put soap at every water point._  

Check question: `e2-48-order` · ordering, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Put the parts of the report in order.

To: the Principal
From: the Health Club
Date: 14 May

Introduction: This report describes how pupils wash their hands at school.

Findings: We visited the school compound during break for one week. There are only two water points for 900 pupils, and there is no soap. Most pupils eat without washing their hands. Three classes had pupils absent with diarrhoea last month.

Recommendations: We recommend more water points, soap at every point each week, and a hand-washing campaign led by the Health Club.

## Lesson 49: Speaking and Listening- Talk about banks, kinds and their services, then listen to a short text on banking

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e2-49 (Talking about banks and their services; listening to a dialogue at the bank)

**Note** (draft)

> Text: Opening an account
> Mr Fonyuy: Good morning. I would like to open a savings account for my daughter.
> Bank clerk: Good morning, sir. You need your identity card, her birth certificate, two passport photographs and a first deposit.
> Mr Fonyuy: Will the money earn interest?
> Bank clerk: Yes, a savings account earns a little interest every year. You can deposit money at any time and withdraw it at the counter or with a bank card at the ATM.
> Mr Fonyuy: Can I also send money to my brother in Garoua?
> Bank clerk: Of course. We do transfers between our branches, and you can also use mobile money.
> 
> Kinds of banks: commercial banks (accounts, loans, transfers), savings banks and microfinance institutions (savings and small loans), the central bank (BEAC, the Bank of Central African States, which issues the CFA franc). Words: deposit (put in), withdraw (take out), loan, interest, ATM.

**Card c49-1** (draft)

> Deposit = put money in; withdraw = take money out.
>
> _She deposited her savings and withdrew some at the ATM._  

Check question: `e2-49-words` · matching, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Match each banking word to its meaning.

Right-hand side (shuffled): to put money into an account · sending money from one account to another · to take money out of an account · money borrowed that must be paid back
- a loan → **money borrowed that must be paid back**
- to withdraw → **to take money out of an account**
- to deposit → **to put money into an account**
- a transfer → **sending money from one account to another**

Full working after a second miss:

> The right pairs are:  
> a loan → money borrowed that must be paid back  
> to withdraw → to take money out of an account  
> to deposit → to put money into an account  
> a transfer → sending money from one account to another  

**Card c49-2** (draft)

> Banks keep money safe, give loans and send money.
>
> _A loan is money borrowed from the bank._  

Check question: `e2-49-text` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Dialogue “Opening an account”: how can he send money to his brother?

- ✅ by a transfer between branches or by mobile money
- ◻️ by giving it to the clerk to carry  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The text does not say that._
- ◻️ he cannot  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The clerk says “Of course.”_
- ◻️ by post only  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The text does not say that._

Full working after a second miss:

> Transfers between branches, or mobile money.  
> So the answer is “by a transfer between branches or by mobile money”.  

**Card c49-3** (draft)

> A savings account earns interest.
>
> _The money grows a little each year._  

Check question: `e2-49-idea-card` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**

- ◻️ a savings bank  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: It keeps savings._
- ✅ BEAC, the Bank of Central African States
- ◻️ the post office  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: It is not a bank._
- ◻️ a microfinance institution

Full working after a second miss:

> BEAC is the central bank.

## Lesson 51: Grammar- Countable and uncountable nouns

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e2-51 (Countable and uncountable nouns)

**Note** (draft)

> Countable nouns can be counted and have a plural: one coin, two coins; a bank, three banks.
> Uncountable nouns cannot be counted and have no plural: money, water, rice, bread, information, advice, furniture, luggage. We do not say “a money” or “informations”.
> • many / a few / few + countable plural: many coins, a few banks.
> • much / a little / little + uncountable: much money, a little water.
> • a lot of, some, any go with both: a lot of coins, a lot of money.
> • To count an uncountable noun, use a unit: a piece of advice, a bag of rice, a glass of water.

**Card c51-1** (draft)

> Countable nouns have a plural; uncountable nouns do not.
>
> _coin → coins; money (no plural)_  

Check question: `e2-51-kind` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Is “money” countable or uncountable?

- ✅ uncountable
- ◻️ a proper noun  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: It is a common noun._
- ◻️ both  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Money has no plural._
- ◻️ countable  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: We do not say “two moneys”._

Full working after a second miss:

> We count coins or notes, not “moneys”.  
> So the answer is “uncountable”.  

**Card c51-2** (draft)

> many + countable; much + uncountable.
>
> _many customers, much money_

> Choose much or many.

Sentence: **How ___ customers are waiting?**
- ✅ many
- ◻️ a little  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: A little goes with uncountable nouns._
- ◻️ much  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Customers is a countable plural: many._

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing word is “many”.  
> How many customers are waiting?  

**Card c51-3** (draft)

> Count uncountable nouns with a unit.
>
> _a piece of advice, a bag of rice_  

Check question: `e2-51-unit-card` · fill the blank, level 1 · **authored key**

> Choose the right unit.

Sentence: **Please give me a ___ of water.**
- ✅ glass
- ◻️ piece  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: We do not say a piece of water._
- ◻️ bag  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Water is drunk from a glass._

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing word is “glass”.  
> Please give me a glass of water.  

**Worked example** (draft)

> Correct: “She gave me many informations and two advices.”

1. information and advice are uncountable: no plural, no “many”.
2. She gave me a lot of information and two pieces of advice.

**Answer:** a lot of information and two pieces of advice

## Lesson 52: Writing-Describe activities carried out at the banks and also write on the importance of banks

Prerequisites: 49, 51 · Skill: e2-52 (Writing about the activities and importance of banks)

**Note** (draft)

> To describe activities: say what happens and who does it, in the present simple (The clerk checks the identity card. Customers queue at the counter.).
> To write about importance: give reasons with linking words: firstly, secondly, also, moreover, finally, therefore.
> Plan: introduction (what a bank is); paragraph on activities (deposits, withdrawals, loans, transfers); paragraph on importance (safety, savings, loans for businesses, paying bills); conclusion.

4. Conclusion: banks help families and the country.

**Answer:** introduction → activities → importance → conclusion

**Paper task** (draft; not marked by the app, mastery stays unknown)

> Write an essay of about 150 words in your exercise book: “The activities and importance of banks”. Use an introduction, a paragraph on activities, a paragraph on importance and a conclusion.

Model answer, shown after the pupil has written:

> A bank is a business that keeps people's money safely and lends money to those who need it.

Every day, many activities take place in a bank. Customers queue at the counters to deposit or withdraw money. Clerks check identity cards and count notes. Some customers ask for loans to build houses or start businesses, while others transfer money to relatives in other towns. Many people use the ATM outside.

Banks are very important. Firstly, money kept in a bank is safe from thieves and fire. Secondly, savings earn interest. Moreover, loans help people to start businesses, which create jobs. Finally, companies pay their workers' salaries through banks.

Therefore, banks help families and the whole country to develop.  

Self-check list:

- [ ] My introduction says what a bank is.
- [ ] I described activities in the present simple.
- [ ] I gave reasons with linking words.
- [ ] I wrote a conclusion.
- [ ] My paragraphs each have one main idea.

## Lesson 53: Speaking and Reading- Discuss in your groups about sporting infrastructure

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e2-53 (Talking about sporting infrastructure; reading about new stadiums)

**Note** (draft)

> Text: New stadiums for Cameroon
> When Cameroon hosted the Africa Cup of Nations in 2022, new stadiums were built, such as the Olembe stadium in Yaoundé and the Japoma stadium in Douala, and older ones were renovated. Besides the main pitch, these sports complexes have training grounds, changing rooms, a medical centre and stands for thousands of spectators. But good infrastructure must be maintained: grass must be cut and watered, seats repaired and toilets cleaned. Many young people also need small facilities near their homes: a basketball court in the quarter, a handball court at school, a running track. "A stadium is not only for big matches," says a coach in Buea. "It is where talents grow."
> 
> Sporting infrastructure means the buildings and grounds for sport: stadiums, pitches, courts, tracks, swimming pools, gymnasiums. They need maintenance (care and repairs).

## Lesson 54: Vocabulary- Kinds of sporting infrastructure or facilities

Prerequisites: 53 · Skill: e2-54 (Kinds of sporting infrastructure and facilities)

**Note** (draft)

> Each sport has its place: football and rugby are played on a pitch (a field); basketball, volleyball, handball and tennis on a court; swimming in a pool; running on a track; boxing in a ring; gymnastics in a gymnasium (gym).
> A stadium has a pitch surrounded by a track and stands. A sports complex groups several facilities. Equipment: goal posts, nets, a scoreboard, floodlights (lights for night matches).
> People: players, athletes, a coach, a referee, spectators, a groundsman (who looks after the pitch).

## Lesson 55: Grammar- Pronouns

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e2-55 (Pronouns)

**Note** (draft)

> A pronoun replaces a noun so that we do not repeat it.
> • Subject pronouns (before the verb): I, you, he, she, it, we, they — She plays handball.
> • Object pronouns (after a verb or a preposition): me, you, him, her, it, us, them — The coach chose her. Come with us.
> • Possessive pronouns (stand alone): mine, yours, his, hers, ours, theirs — This ball is mine. (Possessive adjectives come before a noun: my ball.)
> • Reflexive pronouns (the same person as the subject): myself, yourself, himself, herself, itself, ourselves, yourselves, themselves — He hurt himself.

**Card c55-1** (draft)

> Subject pronoun before the verb; object pronoun after it.
>
> _They watched him._

  _↳ feedback if chosen: Before the verb, use the subject pronoun: We._
- ✅ We

## Lesson 56: Writing- Describe a sports infrastructure. Write an expository essay on sports infrastructure

Prerequisites: 53, 54 · Skill: e2-56 (Describing a sports facility; writing an expository essay)

**Note** (draft)

> To describe a place, go from the general to the details and use place words: in front of, behind, on the left, around, opposite. Use adjectives and the present tense.
> An expository essay explains a topic with facts and reasons, not a story: introduction (the topic and why it matters); body paragraphs (one idea each: uses, benefits, problems, solutions); conclusion.
> Linking words: firstly, in addition, for example, however, as a result, in conclusion.

3. Conclusion: the town should maintain them and build small courts in the quarters.

**Answer:** introduction → body (one idea per paragraph) → conclusion

**Paper task** (draft; not marked by the app, mastery stays unknown)

> In your exercise book, describe a sports facility you know (about 80 words), then write an expository essay of about 150 words: “The importance of sports infrastructure in my town”.

Model answer, shown after the pupil has written:

> Description: Our school sports ground lies behind the classrooms. In the middle is a large football pitch with white lines and two goal posts. Around it runs a red track. On the left there is a basketball court with a cement floor, and opposite it a small shed where the equipment is kept.

Essay: Our town has a stadium and two basketball courts. These facilities are very important. Firstly, they keep young people healthy and away from bad company. In addition, matches bring visitors, and traders sell food and drinks. For example, during the inter-school games the stadium is full every afternoon. However, the pitch has holes and the toilets do not work. As a result, some games are cancelled. In conclusion, our council should maintain the stadium and build more courts in the quarters.  

Self-check list:

- [ ] My description goes from general to details.
- [ ] I used place words.
- [ ] My essay has an introduction, body paragraphs and a conclusion.
- [ ] Each body paragraph has one idea.
- [ ] I used linking words.
- [ ] My essay explains; it does not tell a story.

> The correct order is: Our town has a stadium and two basketball courts. → Firstly, these facilities keep young people healthy and busy. → In addition, matches bring visitors and money to the town. → However, the pitch is badly maintained. → In conclusion, we must look after our sports infrastructure..  

## Lesson 57: Speaking and Reading- Talk about refuse disposal, collection and the challenges involved

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e2-57 (Talking about refuse disposal; reading about rubbish collection)

**Note** (draft)

> Text: Who will collect the rubbish?
> In our town, the council's trucks collect refuse from large bins along the main roads. But many quarters have no bins, so people throw their rubbish into gutters, rivers or empty plots. When it rains, the blocked gutters overflow and flood the houses. Some people burn their rubbish, and the smoke makes children cough. The trucks are too few and they sometimes do not come for a week. Last month, the youths of our quarter organised a clean-up campaign. They cleared the gutters, put up signs saying "No dumping", and asked the council to place a bin at the junction. Now every family carries its rubbish there in a closed bag.
> 
> Words: refuse (rubbish, garbage), a bin, a dump, to dispose of, a gutter, to overflow, a clean-up campaign, biodegradable, recycling.

## Lesson 58: Vocabulary- Punctuation

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e2-58 (Punctuation (1))

**Note** (draft)

> • Capital letter: at the start of a sentence and for proper nouns.
> • Full stop (.): at the end of a statement.
> • Question mark (?): at the end of a direct question.
> • Exclamation mark (!): after a strong feeling or a sharp order: What a dirty street! Stop!
> • Comma (,): between items in a list (paper, plastic and glass), after an introductory word (However, …), around a name in direct address (Ayuk, come here.).
> • Apostrophe (’): for a missing letter (don't, it's = it is) and for possession (the council's trucks, the youths' campaign).

**Card c58-1** (draft)

> A statement ends with a full stop; a question with a question mark.
>
> _Where is the bin? It is at the junction._  

Check question: `e2-58-end` · fill the blank, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Choose the punctuation mark that ends the sentence.

Sentence: **The bin is at the junction___**
- ◻️ ?  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: It is a statement._
- ✅ .
- ◻️ !  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: It is a calm statement._

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing word is “.”.  
> The bin is at the junction.  

**Card c58-2** (draft)

> Commas separate items in a list.
>
> _paper, plastic and glass_  

Check question: `e2-58-comma` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which sentence is punctuated correctly?

- ◻️ We sort paper plastic and glass.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Separate the items with commas._
- ◻️ We sort, paper, plastic, and, glass.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Too many commas._
- ◻️ We sort paper plastic, and glass.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The comma is in the wrong place._
- ✅ We sort paper, plastic and glass.

Full working after a second miss:

> Commas between the items of a list.  
> So the answer is “We sort paper, plastic and glass.”.  

**Card c58-3** (draft)

> The apostrophe shows a missing letter or possession.
>
> _don't; the council's truck_  

Check question: `e2-58-apos-card` · fill the blank, level 1 · **authored key**

> Choose the correct form.

Sentence: **We ___ burn rubbish.**
- ✅ don't
- ◻️ do'nt  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The apostrophe goes where the letter is missing._
- ◻️ dont  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: The apostrophe replaces the o of not._

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing word is “don't”.  
> We don't burn rubbish.

1. Capital A; comma after the name in direct address; question mark.
2. Possession: council's; contraction: didn't.

**Answer:** Ayuk, did you see the council's truck? It didn't come.

## Lesson 59: Grammar- Impersonal pronouns

Prerequisites: 55 · Skill: e2-59 (Impersonal pronouns)

**Note** (draft)

> Impersonal pronouns do not refer to a particular person or thing.
> • it: for weather, time, distance and to introduce an idea: It is raining. It is ten o'clock. It is important to keep the town clean.
> • there: to say that something exists: There is a bin at the junction. There are no trucks today. (there is + singular, there are + plural)
> • one, you, they: for people in general: One should not litter. You never know. They say the council will buy new trucks.

**Card c59-1** (draft)

> It for weather, time and to introduce an idea.
>
> _It is important to sort rubbish._  

Check question: `e2-59-it` · fill the blank, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

Sentence: **___ is raining, so the gutters are full.**
- ✅ It
- ◻️ One  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: One is for people in general._
- ◻️ There  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Weather uses it._

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing word is “It”.  
> It is raining, so the gutters are full.  

**Card c59-2** (draft)

> There is / there are say that something exists.
>
> _There are two bins._

  _↳ feedback if chosen: To say something exists, use there._

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing words are “There is”.  
> There is a big bin at the market.

> One, you, they can mean people in general.

## Lesson 60: Writing- Write a short paragraph about human investment or keeping the environment clean

Prerequisites: 57 · Skill: e2-60 (Writing a paragraph about human investment or a clean environment)

**Note** (draft)

> A good paragraph has one main idea: a topic sentence, supporting sentences (reasons, examples, facts) and a concluding sentence.
> Human investment is unpaid work that people do together for the community, like cleaning a market or clearing gutters.
> Use linking words (first, also, in addition, as a result) and the present tense for general facts.

**Card c60-1** (draft)

> Start with a topic sentence that gives the main idea.
>

**Paper task** (draft; not marked by the app, mastery stays unknown)

> Write a paragraph of about 100 words in your exercise book on “Human investment” or “Keeping our environment clean”.

Model answer, shown after the pupil has written:

> Human investment keeps our quarter clean and healthy. On the last Saturday of each month, men, women and children come out with hoes, brooms and wheelbarrows. We clear the gutters, cut the tall grass and collect plastic bags from the roadside. The youths carry the rubbish to the bin at the junction. As a result, there are fewer mosquitoes and the gutters no longer overflow when it rains. Our quarter is also more beautiful, and visitors notice it. Human investment costs no money, but it needs everybody's effort. Everyone should take part.  

Self-check list:

- [ ] My paragraph has one main idea.
- [ ] I began with a topic sentence.
- [ ] I gave reasons and examples.
- [ ] I used linking words.
- [ ] I ended with a concluding sentence.

## Lesson 61: Speaking and Reading -Talk about progress in science, uses of robots in health - telehealth and then read a text on hospital in my bedroom

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e2-61 (Talking about progress in science; reading about telehealth)

**Note** (draft)

> Text: A hospital in my bedroom
> When my grandfather's heart problem came back, we did not travel to the city. A nurse from the health centre brought a small machine and attached it to his chest. The recording was sent by phone to a heart specialist three hundred kilometres away, who read it within an hour and changed his medicine. This is telehealth: using phones, computers and the internet to give health care at a distance. In some big hospitals, robots now help surgeons to operate with great precision, and computers help doctors to read X-rays. But machines do not replace people: the nurse still came to our house, explained everything and held my grandfather's hand.
> 
> Words: technology, invention, a robot, telehealth (telemedicine), a specialist, a device, at a distance, precision.

## Lesson 62: Vocabulary- Homonyms

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e2-62 (Homonyms)

**Note** (draft)

> Homonyms are words that are spelt and pronounced the same but have different meanings: bank (of a river / for money), light (not heavy / brightness), match (a game / a stick for fire), watch (a clock on the wrist / to look at), ring (jewellery / where boxers fight), bark (of a tree / of a dog), spring (a season / where water comes out).
> To find the right meaning, look at the other words in the sentence (the context).
> Do not mix them up with homophones, which sound the same but are spelt differently (sea / see).

**Card c62-1** (draft)

> Homonyms: same spelling, same sound, different meanings.
>
> _a bat (animal) / a bat (for cricket)_  

Check question: `e2-62-mean` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> What does the word in capitals mean? “She deposited money at the BANK.”

- ◻️ the side of a river  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is the other meaning; look at the context._
- ✅ a place that keeps money
- ◻️ a school subject  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is not a meaning of this word._
- ◻️ a kind of food  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is not a meaning of this word._

Full working after a second miss:

> In this sentence, bank means a place that keeps money.  
> So the answer is “a place that keeps money”.  

**Card c62-2** (draft)

> Use the context to find the meaning.
>
> _“The dog's bark woke me” → the sound of a dog._  

Check question: `e2-62-ctx` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which sentence uses the word “watch” to mean “a clock worn on the wrist”?

- ◻️ Watch the road carefully.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Here watch means look at._
- ◻️ We watch football on Sundays.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Here watch means look at._
- ✅ My watch stopped at noon.
- ◻️ The soldiers keep watch at night.  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Here watch means guard duty._

Full working after a second miss:

> A watch that stops is a clock.  
> So the answer is “My watch stopped at noon.”.  

**Card c62-3** (draft)

> Homophones are different: they are spelt differently.
>
> _sea / see, right / write_  

Check question: `e2-62-kind-card` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**

- ✅ hear / here

  _↳ feedback if chosen: Same spelling: homonyms._

Full working after a second miss:

> They sound the same but are spelt differently.

**Answer:** the side of a river

## Lesson 63: Grammar- the indefinite and definite article

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e2-63 (The indefinite and definite articles)

**Note** (draft)

> Indefinite articles: a / an, for one thing that is not yet known: a robot, an invention.
> Use an before a vowel SOUND, a before a consonant SOUND: an engineer, an hour (h is silent), an honest man; but a university, a uniform, a European (they start with the sound /j/), a one-way street.
> Definite article: the, for something already known or the only one: I saw a robot. The robot was carrying medicine. The sun, the internet, the best doctor.
> No article before most uncountable nouns used in general (Science is useful) or before names of people, towns and countries (Cameroon, Buea).

**Card c63-1** (draft)

> Use an before a vowel sound, a before a consonant sound.
>
> _an hour, a university_  

Check question: `e2-63-aan` · fill the blank, level 1 · computed by rule, reused from practice

> Choose a or an.

Sentence: **The scientist showed us ___ hour.**
- ✅ an
- ◻️ a  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Listen to the first sound of “hour”: it is a vowel sound._
- ◻️ the  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: We do not know which one yet: it is the first time we mention it, so use a or an._

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing word is “an”.  
> The scientist showed us an hour.  

**Card c63-2** (draft)

> Use the for something already mentioned or the only one.
>
> _I bought a phone. The phone is new._  

Check question: `e2-63-the` · fill the blank, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

Sentence: **___ sun gives us light and heat.**
- ✅ The
- ◻️ An  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: There is only one sun: the._
- ◻️ A  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: There is only one sun: the._

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing word is “The”.  
> The sun gives us light and heat.  

**Card c63-3** (draft)

> No article before names of people, towns and countries.
>
> _She studied in Buea, in Cameroon._  

Check question: `e2-63-none-card` · fill the blank, level 1 · **authored key**

> Choose the article, or ∅ for no article.

Sentence: **We use ___ internet to talk to the doctor.**
- ◻️ an  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: We say the internet._
- ◻️ ∅  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: We say the internet._
- ✅ the

Full working after a second miss:

> The missing word is “the”.  
> We use the internet to talk to the doctor.

1. Listen to the first sound: hour starts with a vowel sound (h is silent) → an.
2. university starts with /j/ → a. engineer starts with a vowel sound → an.

**Answer:** an hour, a university, an engineer

## Lesson 64: Writing- Write a biography of any scientist in his invention and discoveries

Prerequisites: 61 · Skill: e2-64 (Writing a biography of a scientist)

**Note** (draft)

> A biography is the story of a person's life written by someone else. It is in the third person (he, she) and mostly in the simple past.
> Plan: birth and early life (where, when, family); education; the invention or discovery (what it is, why it matters); later life and honours; why you admire the person.
> Use time words: in 1847, as a child, later, after that, finally. Check facts in a book or ask your teacher.

**Card c64-1** (draft)

> A biography is in the third person and the simple past.
>
> _She was born in a small village._  

Check question: `e2-64-order` · ordering, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Put the parts of the biography in order.

Shown as: Arthur Zang was born in Cameroon and loved electronics as a boy. · He invented the Cardiopad, a tablet that sends heart tests to doctors far away. · He studied computer engineering at university. · I admire him because he used science to help patients in villages. · His invention has received international awards.
Correct order: Arthur Zang was born in Cameroon and loved electronics as a boy. → He studied computer engineering at university. → He invented the Cardiopad, a tablet that sends heart tests to doctors far away. → His invention has received international awards. → I admire him because he used science to help patients in villages.

> Write a biography (about 150 words) in your exercise book of a scientist or inventor you admire. Follow the order of his or her life and explain the importance of the invention or discovery.

Model answer, shown after the pupil has written:

> Arthur Zang is a Cameroonian engineer. As a boy he loved taking radios apart to see how they worked. He studied computer engineering at university in Yaoundé.

While he was a student, he saw that many patients with heart problems lived far from a heart specialist. He decided to help them. He invented the Cardiopad, a touch-screen tablet that records a patient's heart test in a village health centre and sends it to a specialist in the city.

His invention has won international prizes, and it is used in several health centres. I admire him because he used science to bring care to people who live far from big hospitals.  

Self-check list:

- [ ] My biography is in the third person.
- [ ] I used the simple past for his or her life.
- [ ] I followed the order of the person's life.
- [ ] I explained why the invention or discovery matters.
- [ ] I said why I admire the person.

Correct order: Arthur Zang was born in Cameroon and loved electronics as a boy. → He studied computer engineering at university. → He invented the Cardiopad, a tablet that sends heart tests to doctors far away. → His invention has received international awards. → I admire him because he used science to help patients in villages.

## Lesson 65: Speaking and Reading- Talk about climate change then read a passage on global warming and natural hazards

Prerequisites: none · Skill: e2-65 (Talking about climate change; reading about global warming and natural hazards)

**Note** (draft)

> Text: A warmer world
> The Earth is getting warmer. When we burn wood, petrol and coal, and when forests are cut down, more carbon dioxide stays in the air and traps the sun's heat, like the glass of a greenhouse. This is global warming. It changes the climate: the rains come late or too heavily, the dry season gets longer and hotter, and storms become stronger. In the north of Cameroon, droughts dry up wells and crops; elsewhere, heavy rains cause floods and landslides that destroy homes. We can all help: plant trees, save energy, avoid burning bushes and rubbish, and walk or share transport when we can.
> 
> Words: climate (the usual weather of a place over many years), global warming, carbon dioxide, the greenhouse effect, a drought (a long time without rain), a flood, a landslide, a natural hazard (a natural danger), to adapt.

>
> _Like the glass of a greenhouse._

- climate → **the usual weather of a place over many years**
- global warming → **the slow rise of the Earth's temperature**
- a flood → **a lot of water covering land that is usually dry**
- a landslide → **a mass of earth and rocks falling down a hill**

## Lesson 66: Vocabulary- Syllabification

Prerequisites: 65 · Skill: e2-66 (Syllabification)

**Note** (draft)

> A syllable is a part of a word with one vowel sound. Clap as you say the word: each clap is a syllable.
> tree (1), for-est (2), en-er-gy (3), en-vi-ron-ment (4).
> Count vowel SOUNDS, not vowel letters: “drought” has two vowel letters but one sound, so one syllable; a silent final e does not count (cli-mate).
> Dividing words into syllables helps with spelling and with breaking a word at the end of a line (use a hyphen between syllables: envi-ronment).

**Card c66-1** (draft)

> Each syllable has one vowel sound: clap to count.
>
> _for-est: two claps_  

Check question: `e2-66-count` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> How many syllables are there in “environment”?

- ✅ 4
- ◻️ 3  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Clap the word slowly and count again._
- ◻️ 1  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Clap the word slowly and count again._
- ◻️ 2  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Clap the word slowly and count again._

Full working after a second miss:

> en-vi-ron-ment: 4.  
> So the answer is “4”.  

**Card c66-2** (draft)

> Count sounds, not letters: a silent e does not count.
>
> _cli-mate: two syllables_  

Check question: `e2-66-split` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**, reused from practice

> Which is the correct division of “forest” into syllables?

- ✅ for-est
- ◻️ forest-  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: That is not a division._
- ◻️ fo-rest  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: We say FOR-est._
- ◻️ f-orest  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: A syllable needs a vowel sound._

Full working after a second miss:

> for-est.  
> So the answer is “for-est”.  

**Card c66-3** (draft)

> Break a word between syllables at the end of a line.
>
> _envi-ronment, not envir-onment_  

Check question: `e2-66-break-card` · multiple choice, level 1 · **authored key**

> A word must be broken at the end of a line. Which break is correct?

- ✅ tempera-ture
- ◻️ temp-erature  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Break between syllables: tem-per-a-ture._
- ◻️ temperatu-re  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Do not leave a letter alone._
- ◻️ te-mperature  
  _↳ feedback if chosen: Break between syllables._

Full working after a second miss:

> tem-per-a | ture.

1. Clap: tem-per-a-ture → four claps.
2. green-house → two claps.
